He returned to the story of the writer who was enjoying lunch with his kids at the gas station sandwich shop. They paid for their meal. They were just emerging from the filling station into a cloud covered Texas panhandle parking lot.

His son sat in the passenger seat of the little blue car. Inspired by his new college experience, The middle-aged-man’s son spoke in elevated tones. He spoke quickly and with much energy.

“I’ve been reading some Richard Dawkins,” said the son, “He might not be as much of an arrogant ass as we thought he was, Dad. He’s really got some good things to say.”

The father was searching for common ground. 

“Okay,” said the father, “tell me what you mean.”

The son went on to discuss Dawkin’s book The Selfish Gene, published in 1976 but still required reading in a formal education on biological evolution. The father was proud of his son’s curiosity. He was happy that his son was able to enjoy learning. 

“The common spiritual ground that we shared a while back…I’m not there any more.” Said the son, “I’ve become more of a materialist.”

The son, like the father was being folded over in the blueberry batter, leaving form behind. 

“Well, I’m proud of you, son. You are growing. You are progressing. It’s okay if we don’t have that same common ground right now. I wonder what common ground do we share?”

The two men rediscovered each other through conversation, trying to make peace in this dynamic relationship between two dynamic individuals. 

“I’m worried about you Dad. I’m worried because you don’t have Mom to take care of you any more. Whose going to take care of you?” It was difficult for him to accept the father’s latest decisions to drop his nets and surrender to the flow of life. 

“I’m not ready to go with that flow,” said the son, “I’ve got too much to get done. I’m just not in that place. I’m in school. I’ve got a life to build.”

“I understand,” said the father, “We are at different stages.”

“I mean, I could understand if you were my age,” said the son, “Maybe I could understand you taking some time out. But, I don’t understand you doing what you are doing at this age. “What are you going to do? How are you going to survive? You don’t have anyone to take care of you now.”

“You know, son, when I left your mother I told her exactly that. I told her that it was time for me to learn to take care of myself. I think I’m in that process. I’m learning how to live in a way that suits me. I don’t have the answers to those questions. I wish I could tell you, but I just can’t. I hear your concerns. I am listening.” 

The father continued. “Son, I love you just the way you are. You don’t have to see things my way in order for me to approve of you. You have my blessing. I see you, and I like what I see. You are a young man. I see your heart. You are strong. You are powerful. 

He went on as he wanted to affirm his son, 

“You are building the container to hold the substance of your life (Richard Rohr, Falling Upward). You are in the first half of your life. I trust that you will find your own way. This is a natural process and you are fully engaged. I love you. You have a compassionate, kind, caring, and nurturing heart. You will do what is right for you. I know that you are on the path to your own awakening. Here we are right now, in this moment, awake together. I am so proud of you son. You are so smart and so insightful. So curious. You make me so proud, son. And you don’t have to think like I do to earn my love. Do you hear what I’m saying? You already have my love. You have my unconditional positive regard. You have my blessing. You have your father’s blessing, and I couldn’t take it away even if I wanted to. Because your true blessing comes from within. The true Father’s blessing is one that cannot be revoked. 

The man began to reflect, “My own father has often revoked his blessing for me. It has been a very painful process. He has given me his blessing many times. However, As he encountered his own wounded child within, he tried to revoke his blessing with shame and violence. Your grandfather was never held like he needed to be, son. That still causes him a lot of pain and suffering. So he gives his blessing and then he takes it back. That is hurtful to me. I do not want it to be that way for you. 

My father is a hurting man. He is a suffering man. Because of the years that I have tried to reconcile with my own father, I understand that the blessing I give to you today cannot be revoked. Even if I go crazy and try to take it back someday, this is the blessing that cannot be revoked. Do you hear me, son? It’s okay for you to leave our common ground behind. It’s okay to leave form behind. That’s how we grow.”

“I hear you,” said the son.

They continued their discussion as the father lead them into a discussion about spiral dynamics.

The father breathed into the present moment. It was difficult for him to surrender the common ground that they held several months ago. There was a gap between them emotionally. The father allowed acceptance to rise up into this gap. 

While recognizing the suffering of his own father, The man was still grateful for all that he and all of his ancestors for the gift of life; He was grateful for the opportunity to have this conversation with his own son. Praise be to the ancestors as they have empowered us in our journey up the great snake, the Great Serpent who spirals into the infinite multi-verse.

After several more hours of conversation between the father and son, the two blue cars diverged from the great toll road and onto Eastern Avenue. They turned, one after the other into the winding and wooded streets of his mother’s neighborhood. The son and his children parked their cars along the curb of her home, a red brick house on a hill, by a creek, in a modestly well-to-do neighborhood.

The Universal Mother was preparing for them a great feast. Crackers and cheeses were laid out on the island in her kitchen when they arrived. There were also nuts and grapes and candies available there on the kitchen island. The hospitality of the Universal Mother was palpable in all their hearts, and the son gave much thanks, for his heart was filled with gratitude. The joy of the son’s salvation had been restored unto him. The family rejoiced in each other’s presence long into the night.

A red meat sauce simmered on the mother’s stove, just as it had done long ago, when the son lived with her as a boy, long before the grandchildren were even thought of. 

“It all smells so delicious,” said the son to his mother, “I am so happy to be here with all of you!” he exclaimed. And he truly was.

The Mother made a sacred salad which contained a multitude of textures and flavors. Together, the grandmother and her oldest granddaughter prepared a tangy homemade dressing to compliment the green salad. The mother spooned garlic-butter over a sourdough bread which she then broiled to a perfect shade of golden brown in her oven. 

The family joined hands around the kitchen island. Marl, the mother’s husband blessed the food that was about to nourish their bodies. The son and all the children sat eating and drinking at the mother’s long table that night. They laughed and told stories. They ate until they were full. The son had much joy in his heart. Then he became very sleepy.

The son, giving thanks to his mother for the meal that she’d prepared, got up from the table (even without excusing himself as his mother had taught him to do, but still with much thanks in his heart) and found his way over there to the couch where he covered himself with the weighted blanket, closed his eyes and allowed himself to fall into a sleep as he listened to the laughing and talking of his family. He rose after an hour and rejoined the festivities that were still under way.

The Children were happy, “maybe giddy,” thought the writer. 

Their laughing and joking continued. Trivia games were played. There were somersaults. There were sister-assisted handstands. There was wrestling. There was tickling. The father was recording the festivities with his phone as he recognized all the activities as sacred.


And then there began a great slowing down. Faces were washed and exfoliated. Teeth were brushed. Clothes were retired. Pajamas were employed. Hugs were given. Smiles were exchanged. Good nights were said. The oldest daughter and her husband retired to their room. The two youngest children retired to theirs. The mother and her husband went to their bed too. And the son was left alone in a sacred space on his mother’s couch, this son who was making peace with a Universe who was out to help him.

And so it came to pass that the writer found himself sitting on the couch, the one who recounted all the joy that had transpired on that twenty-seventh day of December in two-thousand-and-nineteen, that year of our Lord, exactly one year after the middle-aged-man had admitted himself to the hospital and been transported by ambulance to Sunrise Mental Health Hospital in Roswell New Mexico. 

But, on this  twenty-seventh day of December of two-thousand-and-nineteen the middle-aged-man was not in a mental hospital. No, dear reader, he was feeling quite well. He sat with great joy, tapping out words on his Chromebook. 

Today he had communicated to his two oldest children the story of how he had calmly declared an emergency, the emergency of the failing engines, the emergency that caused him to set down in the field that he had purchased all those years ago. 

“There it is! There is the field that I bought so many years ago!” exclaimed the father-pilot. He described to his children how he had calmly piloted the plane, how he’d managed to ease the plane down (much like Sully did on the Hudson). “I managed to set her down in the very field that I purchased so long ago, the same field that contained the treasure,” the father proudly explained.

He explained to his two oldest that he was a disciple of Christ, and that he was no longer able to hold the form that their earthly father had maintained all those years. He was able to describe his sufferings in a way that they seemed to understand on some level. The father did not sense that he was justifying himself. But he was very happy to be able to describe to them what was happening in his life. 

Of course, he could not know what his children heard when he spoke. He could not truly understand what they saw when they looked at him. But, the father knew how he had come to the place where he was now. He knew exactly how he had arrived in that field, in all truth and in all honesty. 

The prayer that he had prayed at the cabin in the Lincoln National Forest had been answered. The man had seen the joy of his salvation restored. His heart was full of praise and thanksgiving. He was moving in and out of form. He sat now at his mother’s house in the quiet of the night, the children that he had raised and the husband of his daughter were sleeping in their rooms. His mother and her husband were sleeping in theirs. 

Although he knew exactly how he’d arrived in that field, he felt a satisfaction that had little to do with certitude. He sat with his fingers curled over the home row of his laptop. The laptop rested on the weighted blanket, that same gift that he had said that he wanted even after the gift was already on its way to his daughter.

The writer paused in his writing frequently. He looked around the room. A double sided fireplace opened itself to two separate living areas, one of them adjacent to the kitchen. His couch was located in the main living room where Marl’s great sports television and the leather recliners rested. 

The writer placed his hands together, acknowledging the Life that was being lived through him. He placed his thumbs on his forehead. He bowed his head toward his heart. He allowed himself to come back to the I AM. He gave room for Life to have its say through him.

The man was making peace with a Universe that was out to help him.  He closed his laptop, acknowledging the North. He placed his laptop on the small table by the couch. He went to the bathroom. He returned to the couch. He turned out the lamp. He pulled his weighted blanket over his body. He lay on his back and closed his eyes, giving thanks, giving praise, acknowledging the One Source. He allowed himself to rest. He allowed himself to be the great I AM.

Day 10
5:13AM

The single drop of dew rested upon the wide blade of green grass in the morning sunlight. The yellow light of the sun appeared to emanate from the heart of the dewdrop’s center. In the perfect stillness of the morning the water droplet sat on the green blade of grass which sprung from the earth, rooted in the soil. The drop of dew and the light which shone from its center rested there, in the coolness of the morning mountain air. A stream gurgled just feet away, a world of moving water cascading down the mountain, the inclined earth its impetus. All the unified drops of water flowed together, eddying in places. They carried sticks, formed froth, tumbled playfully over small water falls, and sometimes splashed up on the edges of the bank, then sinking into the soil. 

But the single drop of water which rested on the wide blade of grass was in motion too, inside there was movement that would have been undetectable to the human eye. The dewdrop’s form was maintained only by its own surface tension. This surface tension maintained the smooth and curving surface of the water droplet while the fluid inside the drop maintained movement. This single water droplet sat by the stream of running water. His golden heart was shining. The movement within his form was caused by the heat from the source which caused his heart to glow golden. 

Did this water droplet have desire? Did he wish anything? You may wonder, “Did he wish to join the little playful stream that moved so freely down the mountain? Did this single water droplet wish to join the One-ness of the water in the stream? Did he wish to be free from his position on the blade of grass? 

Can a water droplet wonder? 
Can a water droplet want?
What was in the heart of this little water droplet?
Maybe we should ask him?

Little water droplet, my brother!
You, with the heart of gold!
Little brother! I see the sun shining in your heart.
I see you sitting on your blade of grass.

You are not in motion as your brothers are
But I see the movement within you
I see the sun shining from deep within your being

Do you wish to join the one-ness of the flow?
Do you wish to lose yourself in that great motion?
Do you wish to run down the mountain with your brothers
And sisters?

The water droplet responded

I wish only the will of my father
If it pleases him for me to sit here
On this blade of grass
Then I will

If it pleases Him 
that the sun shines upon me

If it pleases him
to take me up into the sky
one little bit at a time
Then I am pleased

If it pleases my father
That I run down the mountain
With my brothers and sisters
Then I am well pleased, my dear friend

“But now,” said the tiny drop of water
who rested in the morning sunlight 
on the wide blade of grass

Please, stay with me here for a moment
And let us ask for the will of the father.
Together we will trust the father,
That his will be accomplished through us today

Come, sit, my friend
Rest with me for a moment
As the stream flows by over there


And then, the tiny dewdrop, 
in all his wisdom, 
prayed.

The tiny drop prayed 
Then he waited 

For the tiny drop knew that he was not separate from the stream
The tiny drop knew no alienation in himself
As the sun shined from his center
He knew that his current state of being
Was just for this moment

He knew the wisdom of the thunderhead,
For he was the thunderhead
He was the mystery of togetherness
He was the joy of salvation

For it was his simplicity
That made him so pure in heart
It was his simple devotion
That caused him to sit with such patience and acceptance

And so, dear reader, let us sit for a moment
Let us sit with this wisdom
Let us sit here for a moment with our wise brother
The dewdrop 

The dewdrop who knew
That he was no alone

The water droplet who watched the stream
And who was the stream

Come, dear reader, let us sit with our brother
Sit with us here for a moment
Become a water droplet with us
Let us wait for the will of the father.

Selah

The going was slow since the ceremony ended. His subpersonalities were present in his being:

The addict and the escapist were there too. The escapist was trying to find somewhere else to go, somewhere where he didn’t have to feel the pain. The addict thought it might be a good idea to use the medicines to go back to that state of bliss that he’d experienced in the ceremonies.

But there was hope, because the nurturing adult was present too. The nurturing adult was aware of the emotions of all these wounded children, these subpersonalities that were trying so desperately to relieve the pain that they were experiencing.

Day 10
5:48 AM

A young man sat in his senior English class. At the head of the classroom stands Mrs. Hubbard. She is the only one who is interested. With deep gutteral conviction and twist of tongue, the dried lips on her weathered face recite the Canterbury tales. The seniors fall asleep. Saliva falls from the mouth of the weary young man as he wakes up from a nap.

But there is another day, a day when they are not studying the Canterbury Tales. Today they read Walt Whitman. Today the boy-man hears something. He understands. He saw it there. There it was, right there on the page, a meaning so deep that he would not stop repeating the line:

“I lean and loaf at my ease, observing a spear of summer grass.”

The single line of Whitman contained all he needed to know that day. He was moved. Or possibly, his constant anxious motion was arrested for a brief moment that day in his senior English class led by the aging sun-spotted woman. This woman who, day after day, conveyed her love, her passion for these words as she stood up there facing the class, risking the tying of her tongue and the shedding of her tears. She was a tough old woman, tough enough to watch these children come and go. Tough enough to watch these children fall asleep on their desks, and whisper to one another about who-knows-what while she stood up there in front of them, a willing vessel, willing to share the un-sharable to a bunch of dazed and confused seventeen and eighteen year olds.

And so it came to pass that the middle-aged man came to remember his old friend Walt, and his old teacher Mrs. Hubbard. “How long ago has that been now?” the man asked himself. He counted the years. “over twenty seven years ago,” he thought. “twenty-seven years.”

There was a stirring in his mother’s house. The writer, on the couch in his mother’s home, heard the voice of his youngest daughter. He heard the bathroom door open and close. He breathed in and out, several cycles. The toilet flushed. The door opened. Then there was more quiet. The silence of the following moments were a treasure, each breath a last kiss. 

He woke up this morning with Tim on his mind. He wondered if he’d be welcomed in his home. He wondered if he could stand in the presence of the older prophet, his old mentor and teacher. Tim was the man that the middle-aged man had called during his second journey with the mushrooms, the one he’d called Chief. Tim had answered many distress calls from the man over the course of more than twenty years. Now the man wondered if he was still welcome in his home.

The middle-aged-man had been reaching out to Tim recently with texts and a phone call. Tim’s response was minimal. In a voicemail the writer left Tim a simple message. “Hi Tim, I am holding form. Call me when you have a chance.” But Tim had never called. And he was very slow in response to the middle-aged-man’s texts. And when he did respond it was not the type of response that seemed to welcome any further contact. The middle-aged-man continued to reach out occasionally, trying not to let his own fear of rejection dictate how his relationships would go.

Their text exchange had looked like this: (The middle age man found himself afraid to look, afraid of what he might discover about himself if he looked into this history)

November 26th 
Disciple: “That all of them may be one…” John 17:21…Good morning, Chief!...I want to spend some time with you. I will come and play music wherever you are! LoL…Or, I will come and sit quietly wherever you are! I just want to be in your presence, Chief!

Tim: Ok I’ll let you know when I have a meeting

And then, a week-and-a-half later as the middle-aged-man was walking away from his job…

December 5th
Disciple: Hey Tim. I put my two weeks notice in at work. I am sowing myself back into the soil. I have no plan except to let God do what God will. Can I come for a visit? My last day of work is Dec 19th. I have a vision of sitting in your living room.

Tim: I’m leaving town tomorrow not sure what day I’m returning yet. Be home for Christmas. Not sure quitting your job is a good idea. Can you just take a few weeks off for the holidays?

Disciple: It’s already done…I’ll look forward to seeing you I’m not sure it’s a good idea either. I just know that I went as far as I possibly could. I do not have a grace to be in this profession any longer. It is dishonest of me to stay…God will work with me in this I’m sure. I have prayed that everything that is false in me will be burned up…Have safe travels. I wish you well, my friend.

And then ten minutes later, the middle aged man apparently feels that he needs Tim to understand more about what is going on…the distress call continued like this:

Disciple: My “mental illness” and my “calling” are intertwined in a way that I cannot understand. I have been asking for help for years now. I’ve been to 2 emergency rooms, 1 psychiatric unit, and 2 treatment centers. I’ve been asking for help my entire adult life, Tim. I’ve been trying to calmly declare an emergency for weeks now. I have taken this as far as I can come. I’m broken down Tim, and I’m trying to answer my calling in the middle of this. I need help.

I’m asking God to care for me now. I’m asking God to care for my family…I have gone through the appropriate channels. They put me on psych meds that kill my spirit…I’m at the end. I got baptized the Sunday after I talked to you. I am doing my absolute best to surrender my life and will to God. So Tim, please just ask our Father to intervene here. I’ve devoted the last year of my life to prayer and meditation. I’ve been listening. I’ve been following. I’ve been praying every day. I’ve been offering myself to God…The intesity of pain that I experience in my being when I go into these depressions is more than I can work through in this job. I cannot continue caring for others when I am sick in my spirit like this…Can you hear what I am saying? Can you feel what I am talking about? I’m not blaming anyone or anything any more. I’m just hurting beyond what I can bare in the workplace…

1 hour passes

Disciple: okay something happened. My boss didn’t turn in my 2 weeks notice yet. But she says that I’m not fit to continue in my duties. So I’m reaching out to HR and maybe declaring some kind of medical emergency. I may get fired. Maybe I can get disability. I don’t know

Tim: I understand…Praying…

And then, six days later

I’m grateful that I have seen the face of Christ again in my life. Asking God to open and shut the doors, even the ones that are attractive to me

No reply from Tim

And that was their last exchange which had been nine days ago.

As the middle-aged-man awoke on the couch that morning at his mother’s house, Tim was on his heart. And it was in this way that the man found himself, once again kneeling on the floor of the bathroom in the spirit of the dew drop. It is in this way that he found himself at one with the father, saying, “What time is it father?” asking his heavenly father for clear direction. It is in this way that he sought the wisdom of the dew drop, the one who was not afraid to remain on the blade of grass, the one who was not afraid to evaporate into the invisible air, the one who was not afraid to fall from the wide blade of grass and sink into the earth. The dew drop. The one who is at rest. The one who is not afraid. The one with the golden shining heart.

As the middle-aged-man read the texts that he and Tim had exchanged he sensed a desperation in himself. He sensed his own anxiety. He faced an energy in himself, “Or is that an emptiness in myself?” he wondered. “What is this that I am sensing? Is it the little boy who was not held as much or as often as he needed? Am I in touch with that father-shaped-whole in my heart? 

The middle-aged-man wondered why Tim seemed to be withholding himself. The middle-aged-man had been aware of these anxious emotions for weeks now which surrounded his relationship with his old mentor. He had just then found the courage to write about these emotions. The writer wondered if he would find the courage to reach out to Tim, who was only a few minutes away from him now, just across town. “Should I reach out to Tim? He wondered. Tim had been absent in his life for ten years. The middle-aged man had just recently reconnected with him. Now he longed to sit with him again. 

The middle-aged-man shows himself to you now, dear reader. The middle-aged-man who sat in his mother’s living room that weekend after the Christmas of 2019. 

His oldest daughter was awake. The man passed his Chromebook to her lap, sharing with her his writing about the opening of the Christmas presents and the Prince of Egypt caravan the day before. 

The man’s mother was awake now too. She sat across the living room, warming herself by the gas fire, her own tablet in her lap.

Three generations sat there in the mother’s living room. The mother’s oldest typing. The father’s oldest thumbing through facebook on her phone. The middle aged man decided to disengage from his screen and enjoy his family’s presence while he was able. He flipped the screen down. He sat there in the half-lotus, on his mother’s couch, in the presence of these two most important women. 7:05AM

Day 10 
10:20

On that morning, as the man allowed Life to be lived through him, he sang many songs with and for his children. He sang for his mother. He sang for his son-in-law. He shared with his son in law the message of the serpent, the spiral dynamic. 

Who is the enemy?
Satan?
Love your enemy?

“Yes, son. This is where we find ourselves, in this impossible situation.”

This is how he waited patiently for the return of the text that might never come. This is how the disciple acknowledged that father-shaped-hole that was in his own heart. He acknowledged his own insecurities around the man whom he had once called his father in the Spirit. His teacher. His mentor, His spiritual father. So it was that he prepared himself for the next sacred breaking of the heart, and so it was that he communed with God as he offered his friendship, once again to this man who may or may not be interested or willing to meet with him.

The truth was that Tim had never done anything deliberately to hurt the middle-aged-man. When the middle-aged man showed up unannounced at one of Tim’s prophetic meetings just months ago, Tim  immediately invited him to participate in his ceremony, asking the middle-aged-man to retrieve his guitar from his car. He invited him to sing and play without question. So what was this fear that the man was facing around his old friend?

Editor’s note: Above all else I want to see

The middle-aged man had often feared, in the ten years of his absence from Tim, that he himself had been an unholy influence on Tim’s family, that he had brought something unwelcome into his home. The man feared that he was unacceptable in the eyes of Tim’s God. The man suspected that he was feared by Tim and his family. 

Tim now knew about the man’s association with the medicine. The man wondered if Tim considered him somehow disqualified. These are some of his own fears that the middle-aged man was aware of. These are the fears that the man acknowledged as he sat in the presence of his daughter and mother who were just finishing breakfast. The man sat in communion with the clicking of his keyboard, the sound that once belonged to his wife.

“Signifier” and “Signified,” two words that the man had heard his wife use in her study of literary theory. What does a man do when his very name is the signifier of the crossroads? “Is it correct, this signified that I witness in my own being? Is it true that I am one who stands at the crossroads? Is this position at the crossroads somehow deeply connected with my very being? If so, how do I live with myself? How does one stand at the crossroads for a lifetime? Should one stand at the crossroads for a lifetime? Maybe it’s time to move on past the crossroads?”

Many years ago when the young man was away for military duties, he met a man at a mall who was selling bookmarks. The bookmarks were printed with names and their meanings. He found his name among the rows of hanging bookmarks. “At the Crossroads,” was the meaning that he read on the bookmark. The man bought the bookmark. He eventually lost the bookmark, but he still remembered the message.

The writer found himself at a crossroads in his thoughts about Tim. “Will he accept me or reject me?” thought the man. 

What is the function of the man who stands at the crossroads? “What have I learned about this recently?” wondered the writer, “Wasn’t this a thread throughout my recent recorded experiences? Experiencing the BOTH /AND. This is what the Spirit had been showing him to experience when he found himself standing at the fork in the road, presented with the binary. Maybe this wasn’t about acceptance or rejection from Tim. Maybe it was about something different. Maybe it was BOTH/AND. 

What had Christ done when faced with the ultimate rejection that lead to his death? When the Christ was nailed to the crossroads, what had happened? He took it all in. All the pain and suffering, he took it all in. 

“Am I ready to accept rejection from this man who was once a father to me. Am I ready to be corrected by this man? Am I ready to take that in? What about acceptance from Tim? Are you ready for that? The man looked back at his work. His through-line was stretching thin. Would it snap? That’s always a possibility. Some of these deaths that we die go so deep that they blind us. 

Editor’s Note: It became apparent to the editor that a “crossroads” and a “fork in the road” were not the same thing. A crossroads was the meeting of two roads which presented two new directions while still offering the road on which the traveller from which the traveller had ariived. A fork was a bifurcation of the path, only one new possibility.

The writer’s phone rang. He could feel it buzzing in his pants. He stood up from his sitting position on the couch in his mother’s living room and reached in his left pocket to retrieve his phone. It was a California number. It was Dave Workman, of muse harbor publishing. The writer answered his cell phone:

“Hey Dave! Glad you called!

“Hey, how are you? 

“I’m doing very well thanks. I’m just very grateful. I’m here at my mom’s house with my kids. We are having a lovely time together. The writing is coming along, I’ve been discovering what it’s like to be a writer, you know, the mechanics of it along with the rest. How are you, Dave?”

“We are doing pretty good. My son is feeding us all the time. I’m getting ready to go to a princess birthday party today for my granddaughter’s birthday, so who knows what to expect with that.”

“Well, I would love to meet with you tomorrow if you guys are available.”

“Sure,” said Dave, “let’s do it…”

The writer agreed to meet Dave and Eileen on the town square in Bentonville, Arkansas.

As he sat back down on the couch with his laptop, his phone announced a text message with its familiar chime. “I wonder if its Tim,” said the disciple. “How long will I wait to see who that message is from?” he wondered inwardly this time. 

The writer continued his inward interrogation,“Was it Tim? Did it matter? What is hanging in the balance of that question?” He was not sure. The man reassured himself that he was simply making himself available as he allowed this resurrection to continue taking place in his life. “I’m out here in the world,” he said, “I’m learning how to move at the speed of life.”
He was also learning how to say “yes” to what was available. He was learning how to accept. He was learning how to risk, in just the way that he had risked when he saved his life by leaving home twenty months ago. In the same way that he risked when he took the sacred medicine.

The man avoided his phone, not out of dread, but because he was actually enjoying this suspense. He was immersed in this story. He was enjoying this story. And for that, he was truly grateful. 

He was happy to be at his mother’s house with his children. His relationship with Tim was important to him, and he was willing to risk his emotional well being to tend to it. His writing felt exciting to him, and he wanted to see where it would take him. He was willing to give his relationship with his father another chance. He was curious about where his rediscovered faith would take him. He was enjoying his role as the writer, as the middle-aged-man, while not taking himself too seriously. He was holding form, but as loosely as he was able. He had given himself permission to live again. 

Life was out to help him, but that did not mean that he would not suffer. He was prepared for the inevitability that doors would close in his face, and hurt feelings, he knew, may come with that. He was aware that everything would not always go his way. Making peace with a Universe that was out to help him simply meant that Life was there, ready to guide him through the ups and downs. Life was there, ready to help him accept himself through whatever came. He was discovering for himself that Life was creative, loving, kind, and incredibly intelligent, and he was saying “yes” to Life living through him. And yes, that meant that he was curious about the text that had arrived minutes ago. And he was also curious about his thoughts and emotions surrounding the relationship with his old mentor.

He felt into his body. He made peace with the What-Is. He imagined that Schroedinger’s cat was over there in his phone. Schroedinger’s cat in the form of a text message. “Hey, what do you know about Schroedinger’s cat?” he asked his brainy son, certain that he would get a five minute presentation. “Uh, dunno ‘bout Schroedinger’s cat,” said the red headed teenager. 

Strangely enough his son did not know about Schroedinger’s cat. The man thought that strange. His son seemed to know something about most things. “I guess I’m on my own,” thouht the man. The man recalled that there was a cat in a box. And whether that cat was dead or alive depended on something. What was that thing that the cat’s life depended on? “The cat in Schroedingers’ box was somehow both dead and alive until the box was opened. Now how could that be?” he thought. That was the extent of the knowledge the man had about Schroedinger’s cat. He hoped it was enough. Of course, he could have googled, but he felt it unnecessary.

Right now, the message that the man assumed was from Tim was stored in the hard drive on the man’s phone. That message contained some kind of information. The message was a response to a request that the man made. The response was either in the negative or in the positive. “The cat is either dead or alive,” he thought, “Dead or alive a perfect representation of the binary,” he was pleased with himself now. “Maybe it’s not Tim’s response that determines my future. Of course it’s not,” the man laughed at himself, “That seems to be obvious. But how obvious is it, really?” wrote the writer. “Yes, it is obvious to my intellect, but how obvious is it to my heart? How obvious is it to those parts of my being that are scared of rejection? How obvious is it to those wounded children of the psyche, those subpersonalities that Bill Plotkin writes about?”

The time for discussing this was coming to a close. The man knew how to live life, or rather, he was committed to having Life lived through him. There was no amount of preparing for the worst that he could do which could stop him from hurting if is friend rejected him. And there was no certainty that the man would not experience rejection in one form or another even if his friend’s response was in the positive. There were so many opportunities for the man to experience rejection and suffering along the path that he had chosen. He would be knocking on doors and fishing for men the rest of his life. He would have to trust that life was taking care of him. He could sense an uneasiness in his stomach. “Is that just what it feels like to be alive?” he questioned.

The man’s oldest daughter sat down on the leather chair across the room. She looked lovely. “Are we ready?” she said. “I need just another couple of minutes said the man.” He was going out shopping with his family. The man closed his lap top, acknowledged the One-ness by bowing his head toward his heart, and he walked across the room to retrieve his phone.

Day 10
9:38PM

The dying author sat in a reclining chair in one corner of the living room. He was giving room for the miracle to happen again. His fingers were curled over his laptop. His fingers were not moving. 

The dying author fell asleep. Can you see him sleeping there, dear reader? He is asleep there with his laptop in his lap.

The dying author woke up in the chair in the living room of his mother’s home

As he woke, groggy in the chair, the dying author had a vision of the shower shoes that had kept him from coming in contact with the wet floor that day when Drill Sgt Hilliard yelled at him in the shower in Fort Sill, Oklahoma. He was naked. His head was shaved. He could not see well without his glasses on. Those black and white shower shoes were the only thing between the young man and the wet tiles. Drill Sergeant Hilliard screamed at the young man in the shower. It was May of 1996, that thin layer of spongy rubber, the only thing between him and the floor. The young man’s father had also screamed at him naked in the shower.

The weighted blanket comforted the dying author as he sat in the chair. Two large windows would have shown him the backyard had the sun been out, but now there was nothing but blackness. A deep exhaustion had come over the dying author. 

The writer struggled for strength and inspiration. He tried to recount the events of the day.

Tim had finally texted him, the text he’d thought was him earlier had been someone else.

The dying author was unable to form complete thoughts as he lay reclined in that chair in the corner of his mother’s living room by the two windows with the view of darkness. He fell asleep again. He woke from his second nap, still exhaustion was all that he felt. 

The author bought two bibles earlier in the day. He had them engraved with names, one for his mother, and one for his Self. It was a miracle of spiral dynamics that he had bought a bible for himself as he could never have imagined that he would ever buy another bible again in his life. He wondered if he should have had another name beside the one that he went by inscribed in the bible. “Maybe a new name for myself is in order,” he thought, “Perhaps the name, ‘Flow’ instead of the name his parents had given him,” he thought. “Flo, a female name, short for Florence.”


A college football game played in the next room where his bed was. Marl was watching the game. “If you need to go to bed I can watch this in another room,” hollered his stepfather.

There was a beautiful young man with long flowing hair playing quarter back for one of the teams. They kept showing studio pictures of him holding a football. He had long hair and cute smile. He was a gorgeous young man. As she passed through the living room the writer’s youngest daughter stopped for a moment and looked at the television screen as the quarterbacks image appeared, “He looks like a lion, Dad. That man looks like a lion.” The writer agreed. He did look like a Lion, like a gorgeous lion. The middle-aged-man wondered how a young man could look so much like a football player, and so much like a beautiful woman, and so much like a Lion at the same time.

The text that the writer eventually received from Tim was not a rejection. Tim was sick, but he said, “I look forward to seeing you later in the week…” The middle-aged-man again felt into his body as became curious about the nature of those insecurities surrounding his friendship with Tim.

Jason, an old friend from highschool called the writer in response to a text he’d sent earlier that day. The middle-aged man was taking this opportunity to connect with some old friends. Jason was the leader of the young man’s band from highschool. Most of Jason’s other friends in highschool had called him Breed, on account of his last name. But the writer still intentionally called him Jason. They made tentative plans to get together to talk and play music. 

In his first semester at University, the writer’s brainy son had just completed a course entitled the Legacy of Darwin’s Great Idea. As a result, Darwin and Richard Dawkins were very present in much of the conversation that evening. The writer’s mother got out her copy of The Selfish Gene. She had written a paper over the book in her studies for her master’s degree. The writer’s son and his mother talked intensely of evolution. Intensely was just about the only way that his son ever talked, which reminded the writer a lot of himself when he was truly engaged in life.

As a result of this conversation, the writer saw his mother in a different light that evening. He saw a facet of his mother that he’d never been aware of. She was educated in a way that he’d not understood. She held the interaction with her grandson so thoughtfully and gracefully. She was the Universal Glow. They had once called her G-Lo, short for grandma Lorri, and a parody of J-Lo (Jennifer Lopez). But today, the writer had seen her as the “Glow,” (G-Lo). And now the “Universal Glow.”

The writer felt no through-line tonight. But he’d read enough novels to know that sometimes the narrator just kind of hung out in the space that his characters occupied. And that’s how he felt about life right now, like nothing in particular needed to be happening. “This is just life going on, and it’s just real fine. There’s the through-line, right there,” he thought, “Life is happening and if that’s not doing it for you then I guess you can skip ahead to the next chapter, but this is how the writer’s life was just then.”

He longed for the football game in the next room to end and for silence to fall upon the house. He longed for the silence and the focus that he found at the adobe house.

The writer continued his recounting of the days events:

The middle-aged-man called his friend Dan. That night, on the phone, he found himself held in their friendship. They had a common language that was being born in the restoration that had been occurring in each of their lives. The two friends encouraged one another. They gave thanks for the miracles that they were each witnessing in and around their own lives. They gave thanks for the miracles that they were seeing because of one another’s presence each other’s lives. They acknowledged the presence of God in their midst. They acknowledged the weave, the fabric of the cosmos that held them. The middle-aged-man was truly grateful for his friend. 

The man shared with Dan how he had bought a bible for himself and for his mother that day, a miracle of the great snake which spirals upward, the spiral dynamics, the DNA of human consciousness which moves ever outward into the great multiverse. Today the man was able to say, “Thank you, Jesus,” a truly strange miracle in the life of a man who had forsaken that name years and years ago (just as Petra had) as he understood that the man from Nazareth was never called Jesus (It was something more like Yeshua). But, that morning, as the man spoke those words, “thank you, Jesus,” there was a simple acknowledgement of his own roots, a nod toward his place of material origin, a nod toward the form which had first held his conscious recognition of the Christ. The young man had walked away from religious fundamentalism before he even knew what fundamentalism was. He was moving toward an all inclusive Cosmic Christ before he even knew that Christ could be that open. This is how all almost every conversation went with Dan. Full of joy. Full of life. Full of inspiration.

The man ended his phone call with Dan. He sat in prayer and meditation. His mind was taken back to the months after he and the mother-of-his-children sold all their posessions: 
The writer gave thanks for the Universal Mother, Maryanne Fite, who had said yes to him and his family all those years and years ago as they were buying that field, the field which the pilot would one day set that plane down in—the plane with the failing engines. The field was the one that he and his wife had bought all those years ago. 

He and his young family poured those Sonic drinks out onto the winter wheat which had been planted by the young man and his father-in-law. The family of 3 stood in the field of winter wheat. The young family who loved one another so much. The young family who would soon be 5. 5 would be their count. The husband and wife would hold that form long enough for the children to grow up. And then the husband and wife would let go of that form in order that new form could be born. They would experience a death and a resurrection. 

Thanks be to the God of our youth. Praise be to the Snake, the one who deceives us all that we may experience life. Praise be to the one who deceives, our enemy, whom we love. Dare I say, Praise be to Satan? Praise be to God. Praise be to the Source. Praise be to the Christ who is the ALL and every Thing. In Christ there is no Holy or Profane, all is the Sacred. All is the miracle. There is no matter or non-matter that is held together without the All. Praise the all inclusive. Give thanks for the theosis, the realization of the One-ness in ourselves. Praise be to the Poet who speaks forth that which is not as though it was. Praise be to the Wios, the One who walks the earth.

The middle-aged-man had a sense that he needed to ask Tim about the Wios when he met him next. The writer googled the word but found nothing. The man remembered Tim, years ago, talking about the Wios (the mature son who walks the earth).

As the writer wrote, the domestic activity slowed. The house was quieted. It was 11:46PM on Saturday night. He breathed in relief at having finally made it to Oklahoma City. He remembered his poorly planned hitchhike. He felt that his pilgrimage had come to a conclusion. The pilgrimage that had started weeks ago when he put on his backpack, made a cardboard sign, strapped a mandolin to his pack, put on his yellow elk skin glove, and walked out of the college-cow-town by the strength of his own two legs and back.

He sat in the stillness, breathing it in. He made his way to the bathroom, where he arranged the blue mat and assumed his North facing position. He kneeled into the water droplet pose. “What time is it, father?” he asked. He remained there in that position until he found Life moving him forward to where he was not sure. He would try to get some more sleep. He had a long car ride to Bentonville tomorrow to meet with Dave and Eileen (Eileen and Dave) face to face. “It would be nice to get some good sleep,” he thought to himself.

Praise be to the One who gives sleep. Praise be to the One-Salvation. Amen.

Day 11
3:04AM

Just minutes ago, the writer awoke with a start. He had a dream that he could not remember, but the powerful energy of the dream lingered. The writer paused now, closing his eyes, allowing the energy of the dream, which still lingered in his body, to aprehend him. “The writer paused now,” The writer repeated to himself, for it was in this sentence, “The writer paused now,” that he detected the Great-Turning, or the Great Folding-Over which he had first experienced in his mother’s blueberry batter, and which he had more recently become familiar with after he’d consumed the large dosage of mushrooms on Christmas day. And now he knew what it was that he had dreamed of. It was the dream of this Great Turning to which he awoke with a start when he heard his daughter open and close the bathroom door close just a few minutes ago. “There it was again,” thought the author, “The place where the past meets the present—that place where we perceive the past to be the present through the use of symbolic language?” 

“The writer paused now.”

A statement in the past tense which contained the present.

This reminded him of the Course In Miracles lesson that he’d read and practiced last night just before he went to sleep. “Let’s see, what was the lesson?” wrote the writer. He bent over. He reached down into the space between the couch and the coffee table to retrieve the thick blue book. He retraced the last three days:

Lesson 9: “I see nothing as it is now.”
Lesson 8: “My mind is preoccupied with past thoughts.”
Lesson 7: “I see only the past.”

The writer was reminded that by the time the light from the lamp reflected off the objects around him, travelled to his eyes, and was then processed and interpreted by his nervous system, there was a certain fraction of a second that had passed. What it was that he thought he was seeing now, was actually the past. Yes, it was this lesson that was taught to him once in a physics class. That lesson was taught in the past, that place that we have such a difficult time  escaping. That place where we just cannot help but go.

“The writer wrote now,” wrote the writer.

The writer, by his very nature, is the observer who comments on the past, no matter how infinitely close he may perceive himself to be located in the present moment. “Infinitely close,” repeated the writer to himself. This reminded him of a few lines of a  Rumi poem he’d read last night. 

He remembered that the poem was on page seventy-two and seventy-three of the book that he’d purchased yesterday while shopping with his children. He now felt around for the book between the couch and the coffee table. Was he feeling for Rumi, or was he feeling for the book? Rumi was the author who died so that his reader could live. In his poems his reader found his own life-- Rumi and Hafiz, those Sufi poets who recorded their love affair with the Eternal Now.

The spirit of Rumi Spoke in tongues:

“The moon has opened its face
And its wings made of light.

Borrow eyes to see this,
If yours cannot.

Night and day an arrow comes toward you
From a hidden bow.”
		Rumi (translation Barks)

The writer reached now for the thick blue ACIM book. He practiced his lesson of the day:

“I do not see this book as it is now,” he said.
“I do not see these hands as they are now.”
“I do not observe these thoughts as they are now.”

This writer—the dying author-- commented on the present which he wondered if he could ever truly be a part of. “Even when I am not writing, how close can I really come to the present? Infinitely close? like the arrow? Is this the purpose of the teachings of the Buddha? Isn’t this the purpose of the Vipassana practice?” The writer then paused and practiced ushering himself along in this present moment. His fingers instinctively paused, hovering over home row, allowing his documentation to be as closely in touch with the present moment as possible. 

The writer paused again, his hand was on the Rumi book. He grasped the book. The book: sacred written form which the Christ did not feel necessary to participate. He wrote no words. He was making contact with the people. Christ become a parable himself, his life recorded by others long after he was gone. Legend. 

Christ became the One around whom the myths were told. His life was a hero’s journey, the one-story with which Joseph Campbell and Carl Jung found themselves dancing around. This was the hero’s journey that was now being revived in the writer’s own heart. “Can the parable write of itself?” The writer wondered. 

The writers’s eyes finally came back to Rumi’s living words. He reached across the coffee table to retrieve the Christmas gift he’d received from his only son, the uniquely crafted piece of stained wood with which he could hold a book open with one hand. He read the line about the arrow:

“Night and day an arrow comes toward you
From a hidden bow.”

The writer is transported back to a Spring morning in 1990. He sits in Algebra class. The classroom windows are cracked open. The infectious Spring leaks into the classroom. Birds build nests in the trees on the other side of the high school football field. Mr. Watters stands behind athe overhead projector factoring a binomial. Mr Watters is a fluid father of symbols. 

Two students walk across the parking lot. They each hold a cigarette in their hand. They will not go to any more classes today. Once clear of the parking lot, they stop and light their cigarettes. For a brief moment, they know true freedom.


The overhead projector fan drones on, a tanpura inviting improvisation. His students shift in their seats., sensing the truants freedom as they light their cigarettes. Mr. Watters attunes the break in his student’s attention. He moves his dry erase marker to the top-left quadrant of the overhead projector screen. Without a word, he draws the infinity symbol. Now, he traces the infinity symbol, over and over again as the infinity symbol so naturally invites. 

Obed Watters, projects the infinity symbol in real time on the screen. His job is to teach Algebra, but right now he pauses from the binomial to trace the symbol. The symbol is right there for all of his students to witness. The symbol has no apparent connection to the day’s lesson, but here it is. It is the only thing now in the students minds. The dry erase marker dances with the infinite. 


In another version of our universe which the writer imagined to be only slightly more dystopian than the one in which he now lived, Orwell’s thought-police enter Mr. Watter’s classroom door. They do not knock. Yes, it is the thought police. They come in and sweep up ol’ man Watters. 

Now they are taking pictures of the evidence. “This was an obvious attempt to infect the minds of the children under his charge,” said the lead inspector. 

“Was this an act of subversion?” the writer now questioned, “He seemed such a straight laced man! This man-of-Watters who traced the infinity symbol. Did he open up a hidden and dangerous universe for those boys and girls in the public schools?”

The overhead projector is still wet with the infinity symbol. “This dry-erase marker was purchased with state funding! This marker was purchased exclusively for the purpose of teaching Algebra II!” shouted the man taking pictures disgustedly. 

Old Obed Watters was mopped up. The students are pretending that they are not affected by this sudden intrusion. A gloved hand picked up the marker and placed it in a plastic bag. The high school Juniors sat nervously, waiting for a replacement teacher. They remained very still in their seats. The temportary teacher arrived just minutes after the removal of the excessively fluid Watters. 

The high school students did not mourn the loss of their teacher. They could not mourn as they did not yet know how much they loved him. The infinity symbol was erased forever from the overhead projector, Mr. Watters never seen again. The image however, was forever etched in the student’s collective Soul. That which points to the moon, will have its effect. And at least one of these students will love him one day.

That signifier was having its effect now in the life of the middle-aged-man. Yes, the symbol was having its effect as it pointed to uncountable possibilities in an infinite multiverse. The memory of the symbol penetrated the fabric of those young malleable souls who had witnessed the tracing of the side-lying figure-eight. 

The infinity symbol was not the only introduction made by Watters. It was the same fellow, the middle-aged-man’s Algebra II teacher, who had also introduced him to Zeno’s arrow. The arrow which, by approaching half the distance to its target with every movement, could never actually reach its mark.  The arrow may come infinitely close but never hit its mark. 

This was the same arrow, the writer now believed, that Rumi referred to—this arrow that “night and day comes toward you from a hidden bow.” Rumi asks his reader to surrender to the arrow of which we have no shield to protect ourselves. 

The writer now understood this impartation of Mr. Watters to be of the same variety as his vision of the blueberries—the blueberries that his mother (that Universal Glow) had taught him to fold (not stir!) into the batter. These are the modes by which the writer now understood Life to be communicating with him. This was The Great Learning, The Great Turning, the Great Folding Over, the way by which we evolve. This is how we move forward as a species.

We are not really a static species, you see. Things are not nearly as solid as we think they are. Thank you, Mr Darwin, for showing us this! There’s a way of looking at all life as a transmuting expression of consciousness, the same way that Special Ed, the Marxist, had taught the middle-aged man to see the thunderhead. Any one species, like the clouds or the dewdrop, is in constant flux, although we still feel compelled to reify that which is constantly changing. It’s how we desparately want our world to be. Unchanging. Change can be scary, and evolution presents to us a dynamic world.  

Do you, dear reader, recall those reified beans? Do you remember the Marxist—whatever that means—and the reified beans that he so slyly called the middle-aged-man’s attention to? Those reified beans, our neighbors just South of the U.S. border. Those ones which were alienated from their own labor. Do you remember the beans, dear reader?

It was the train tracks that did it, or so the Marxist said, and it was quite a convincing argument. Yes, the middle-aged-man had come to believe that it was the train tracks that did it—those train tracks that arrived from the North just before the turn of the twentieth century. 

You see, dear reader, the trains brought a great alienation to the land. Alienation, the most dreadful of all diseases. The reified beans were alienated from their own labor. Alienation, that disease of separation. That ailment which is the inability to see the All in one and the one in All. That disease which separates us from our own evolving consciousness. 

Praise be to Mr. Marx! Praise be to Mr. Ed, that sly Californian who brought his subversive impartation to the Eastern High Plains so that the middle-aged-man could add it to his collection—the infinity sign, Zeno’s arrow, the great thunderhead, the reified beans, the train tracks South, and his dear mother’s blueberry batter.

It was the blueberries of which he dreamed at 3AM on his mother’s couch when the sound of his daughter opening the bathroom door woke him. He dreamed of the Great Folding, the same Great Folding which the man feared his consciousness might never return from on that Christmas morning, the same morning that he’d become an infant and longed for nourishment from a Universal Areola. 

It was then that he understood his deep desire to be held. He understood that he was never held as much as he needed. It was in his longing for the Universal Breast in which he understood more clearly his need to be held. And he found the nourishment in his own thumb that morning just a short while before he purged in the bathtub. 

This same man acknowledged his own need to evolve into the Universal Man. The writer is writing. Can you see him, dear reader? There he is. He sits cross-legged on his mother’s couch engaged in an attempt to write the Universal story. There he is. Can you see him as he does his part to invite the Cosmic Christ to arise from within his being?  

This writer now nobly engages himself in this process of death and resurrection. This writer now answers the Universal-Calling of His-Life. He speaks of the One who spoke in Parables. He allows himself to become the Parable. Is it ridiculous? 

Is it foolish that the man bought that proverbial field so many years ago? Was it foolish that the man crash-landed his plane in that field? To the middle-aged-man these actions seemed the most appropriate expression of his own being. These ridiculous actions have become the most meaningful stories in his life.

Who is this writer who says yes to his own death for the sake of his reader’s awakening? How does It happen that we come to see the Parable in the stories of our past? Can we see the Parable in the life of Watters and the life of the Mother, dear reader? Can we see the Parable in the folding of the blueberries? Can we see all those little berries providing sustenance to the evolving consciousness? Can we see our dynamic human nature? 

Can we see that our nature is not static? Is it true that our gender is not as solid as we were told it had to be? Are we making peace with a feminine spirit which arises in a male body, i.e. the Christ. Can we do that, boys? Oh, my! 

Praise be to the One Watters! Praise be to the one who stood in front of us all, tracing the infinity symbol with the dry-erase marker! Praise be to the Universal Fan of the overhead which drones, an invitation to create novel improvisations. Praise be to the Universal Author, fingers curled over home row. He breathes in, an effort to bring the present moment as close to these words as possible. He breathes out now with the same intent. Can you hear him breathing, dear reader.

Praise be to the invisible One-Source from which we all flow. Praise be to the God who is available for the making of Love. Praise be to the hands that crafted the Great Mandolin and the Ude and all the stringed instruments from which our hearts are awakened to the Great Potential of Life. Praise be to the ears that hear the Great Sound. Praise be to the birds, and to the sun, and to the blue sky in which they find their dynamic expression. Praise be to Isaac Newton, the man who described and predicted the movement of the planets while searching for hidden messages in sacred scripture. Praise be to the Breath which is breathed by the ever expanding and contracting Universe. World without end, Amen. Amen.

Selah

The writer writhed slightly in his seat, allowing the Great Sister Serpent, the Kundalini rising to make her way up through his spine. He said “yes” to the the Great Turning as his chakra wheels went round and round within his being. He said “yes” to the beauty of the rainbowed female snake who had made an invitation to him on that Christmas morning. 

His vision of the Snake became meaningless as he tried to save his life from a psychotic death which he was convinced was eminent. On Christmas morning, the man back-pedaled away from this death. Then he engaged himself fully in the saving of his own life by grabbing the guitar which served as his life preserver. He came as close to drowning that day as he ever wanted. He clung to the guitar desperately. He waited for the storm to pass.

The writer paused in his efforts. He had no way of knowing if his task was legitimate (meaning publishable). He could only know the legitimacy in his own being. He longed to be a real boy, to be transformed from a wooden impersonation of a human being into a clearer representation of Essence. It had become apparent to him that there was a falseness in his being that could not move forward with him. There was a suffering within that needed to be relieved. There was something in him that needed help, and these words now seemed to be the most honest expression of that cry for help.

He sensed a greatness in his own being in the writing of these words. He realized, however, after years of sensing that same greatness in the music that flowed from him, that there was no guarantee of notoriety or financial success. The work that he did now was to show him his own greatness. He sensed that. It was possible that it was great for him only. Or maybe it would be a great unknown prayer for those of us who are just out of reach, for those of us who are imprisoned and unable to commune with the soil.

The writer thought of his son in law with whom he had recently connected with. He was sleeping now in the same home in which he now channeled his own healing through this personal message of hope to himself. 

The Son-in-Law, this beautiful being who had said “yes” to the leaving-of-form. The one who had left the God of his youth. The Son-In-Law whom the father-in-law had blessed yesterday with that irrevocable blessing, the blessing which gives its approval in the simple acknowledgment of the Life that is witnessed within. 

The father-in-law spoke the father’s blessing which cannot be taken away. The father’s blessing, the blessing that simply awakens the younger man to his being-ness. The blessing that is the acknowledgment of the I AM, that which already exists in the one who receives the blessing. The blessing that merely points the young man to to THE ONE. The beautiful son-in-law who was inheriting the rising spirit of the times within himself, the convergent evolution of Being arising from within.

The middle-aged-man was grateful to have received the gift of Presence from his children this weekend. He  had spoken with each of his children this weekend. The father sat on his Mother’s couch, fingers curled over home row, once again a with-ness to this miracle of creation. The man said “yes” to the Universal. The man said “yes” to that Which-Is, that which flowed through him. Praise Be to the Great I AM. Praise be to What-Is.

Day 11
6:04AM

Having once again been ravaged by the love of his Lord (Hey! Give me a break, a man has certain needs you know!), the middle-aged-man sat naked on the blue bathroom mat, his back against the West wall. The man wondered if his sense of direction in this neighborhood was true. There was much winding of the neighborhood streets to finally find his mother’s home on Two Bridge Drive.

As he sat in silence, communing with the What-Is, he recalled an email exchange with Harbor Dave the morning after his trip with the medicine in the mountains where he’d witnessed the Christ in the dust of the Universal Moth. Praise the One who is drawn toward the Light! Praise the One Light which draw all Moths. He retrieved the email exchange. He copied and pasted the exchange into the Universal Manuscript, the one being written by the dying author. 

And so it came to pass that the man with the yellow, elk skin gloves (Praise be to the mother Elk!) sat at the table in the Universal Kitchen of the One Cabin in the Universal Woods from which he would emerge a more whole being. Praise be to the Universal Father who makes peace with his Universal Child!

They do say that the link between writer and reader is a two-way street, and I suppose it is. But the writer's in the driver's seat. The reader's just a passenger. Doesn't mean the reader doesn't have power. The reader can always get off at the next exit if the road's too bumpy. So, in a sense, not only does the writer have all the power, but the lion's share of obligation to keep the relationship thriving. To keep the reader comfortable in his/her seat. I watched a moth last night, Dave. It's wing dust was smeared all over my blanket. I'm sure that I must have crushed him. I watched this moth in his dying moments. I witnessed his death. 
 
I wanted to touch him, but I knew that my fingers were too big. There was no possible physical interaction that I could possibly have with this moth. He reached out with a quivering antenna. then he began moving, very slowly, up the canyon of this soft red blanket. He moved almost imperceptibly slow. I knew that I could not leave him. I wondered how long this might take. How much could I take? He was like the Christ carrying the cross up the hill. I think he WAS the Christ. He eventually stopped his forward movement. He became very still. And as I examined him as closely as possible, as I did some kind of close-reading of this beautiful, dying creature, I saw the face of a cat. It reminded me of my cat, Eli, at home. There was some kind of glowing, like spider eyes in the forest when you shine your flashlight on the ground, a sparkling. And then he was gone. The life passed from him, and the cat's face was no longer there. 
 
Moments after, I finally turned out the light to try and go to sleep. I heard a fluttering. A moth came and buzzed my ear, and he grazed my arm, and then I could hear him fluttering against the window shade. Then his sound would disappear, and I could hardly live with not knowing when this moth might return and scare me. But, I did live with it. Somehow I lived with it. 
 
I was so deep in the soil last night that I wondered if I would ever return, the soil that the fungus made by pushing in its tendrils and busting up the rock. Hi Dave

I am very grateful for your investment in this happening in my life. We all have forms that we gather around, fires around which we dance. And I am understanding the writing to be the fire around which you and I are gathering. 

I understand your perspective as a writer and publisher. When I first began teaching music, I was always trying to teach people to take music just as seriously as I did. It was the only way I knew how to teach. After years of pushing people too hard, I realized that if I could just help people to enjoy their instrument, then I was doing them a great service. Not everyone, I finally realized, was going to take this as seriously as I do. What I hear you saying is that you see potential in my voice, but you are also giving me the liberty to maintain this form as something more personal. 

I journaled consistently for about 10 months this last year. At some point I realized that my best "journaling" was being done in the form of these long text messages that I was writing to people, so, I just started copying them into my journal. Through that, along with my mushroom email, I am seeing an important discovery. It is time for me to write with an audience in mind. I will try to not make any assumptions about what that means. However, I find the response I got from the "audience of Dave" to be very encouraging.

I was especially moved by your last comment about things fitting together like puzzle pieces. I felt that.

I was reading an excerpt from Eileen's Raindrops of Love yesterday. I cannot find it right now, but there is someplace where Life is saying something to the effect of "you have been asking this question: Who Am I?....etc...but the real question you need to be asking yourself is 'what can I bring forth into this world which will serve best for the continued arising of consciousness?'" Basically, "how am I best equipped to serve?"

There are so many writers. So many books. So many musicians. So many albums. So many songs, Dave.

As I sit here on the top of this metaphorical mountain, cradled in the branches of this tree, listening to the wind and trying not to put any words in its mouth, I can't help but wonder. I have put my life on a little pause. I am asking Life to create a life for me that is better than I have been able to create. All my creation has dissolved before my eyes, and now I am unafraid. I have been granted the courage to walk into some great unknown. I have heard hero's stories like this.

Back to the fire and the dancing...or the practice of this form. I realized from your response that I have no desire to re-write my autobiography. If something else is to come, it must be something new, a re-approaching of what could be called "my life". I think Stephen Schettini told me everything I needed to hear about the draft of my first autobiography. I think that the "miracle that he asked for must be done through me, not by me.

For years I have toyed with fiction. I wrote the backstory for a Dungeons and Dragons character once (A young marshal artist monk named Jet-Ki..haha). I really enjoyed that. Also, I have brought mythical creatures into parts of my autobiography as a way of dealing with hurt that was unbearable. I also understand that any attempt to write non-fiction is bound by my inability to really tell the truth. In that sense, fiction seems like a much more suitable format for approaching truth. 

I heard you say that there is a mountain story here. That makes sense to me. I understand taking a pack up a mountain. I understand what it is to feel like prey, to wonder if I am being stalked. I hitchhiked to Amarillo last week. I climbed a small mountain on Sunday. I baptized myself (again) in a cold mountain stream as a prayer to become a disciple of the universal Christ. These are the honest ways that I am interacting with the earth recently.

I always struggle for authenticity when I am addressing an audience. I've been performing musically for most of my life. My favorite performers are the ones who seem to be lacking the flavor of performance. It has been my desire to simply allow myself to be an authentic human being who just happens to be holding a guitar and singing on stage. I'd like my writing to be this way. In the past I have experimented with posting my writings on facebook, using the writing to make connections. 

The last word of the previous pararaph: "connections"....That is the purpose of the writing to me. I have seen that I must realize my space in the fabric of community. The manifest connections at this point are the essential part of this journey for me, the system of support. This is an authentic path, to write with the intent of connecting with my fellow human beings. I am sending out little radio signals, little radar pings, bat or dolphin echolations, searching the skies and the seas for other beings.
