And it was in this way that the writer acknowledged the encouragement that he’d received from Harbor Dave of Muse Harbor Publishing. It was in this way that the writer felt himself being held in the arms of some Universal Big Brother (No. Not the kind from Orwell’s 1984!). The writer felt himself being held in the arms of his brother, Dave.

Dave had encouraged the budding writer to expand on the Exquisite stillness that he had found in the mountains. The Exquisite stillness was the man’s first documented mountain-top experience, a mountain story in which there were no mushrooms involved. There was only an uphill path, and a pack, and a fire, and a Forest…and fear…There was fear. And then there was the making of peace with that fear. And, oh yes, there was a cougar. The man believed her to be a mother cougar, which may or may not be expounded on later.

And so it came to pass that the writer cut and paste this first mountain story into this Universal telling of his own story, because (there is that “because” again) the Exquisite stillness and the telling of old stories seemed quite pertinent in this moment. “Because” he was trusting this process. He was trusting Dave and Eileen in the editing process that was on the far horizon. He cut and paste this little mountain story, holding on loosely to the outcome, wondering if it had a place. It did have a place. It had its place in this draft, in all its draftiness.

Exquisite Stillness

exquisite—extremely beautiful and delicate. highly sensitive and discriminating.

	My father is a very fearful man. Of course, he would never admit it. But his fear has eroded a deeply grooved path in his being. Like water which runs through a canyon, my father has no choice but to follow the course that fear has carved through the terrain of his inner person.
	My father has never learned that he is not exactly like his thoughts. He is completely identified with every thought and emotion that he experiences. That’s why he is so easily offended. If his thoughts or emotions are challenged he has no choice but to become defensive and angry.
	My father suffers deeply. But he has hidden his suffering so well from himself that he cannot understand why he feels the way he does. Because he cannot ease his own suffering he must blame people and circumstances for the way he feels. 
	Just last year I asked my father to come and help me. I was going through a difficult time with a recent divorce. My kids weren’t speaking to me. I was plagued by suicidal thoughts. My father was very helpful.  He helped me to make the home where I was staying livable. When I asked him for money that he didn’t have, he became very angry, telling me, “You are the reason that I am a bitter old man. I have dreamed of putting a bullet in your head. And I have dreamed of putting a bullet also in the head of your mother.” His eyes were red and tearful and full of rage as he pointed his finger at me from across the table. Although I was also suffering, I was able to ask him to forgive me for the pain that I had caused him.
	My father does not have friends. He told me that he has intentionally pushed people out of his life and that he experiences satisfaction when his birthday passes and no one acknowledges him. He has also told me that he is hurt when no one acknowledges his birthday. He has said both things to me. I think I understand how both can exist in separate moments.
	It is difficult for me to relate to my father. I know that if I challenge his thoughts and emotions there will be conflict. It seems that merely presenting my own experience challenges him, so I often just let him talk and talk about the way he sees the world, knowing that it will be easier if I just stay quiet.
	
	I am beginning to see how I too, have lived in fear. I am beginning to see how my suffering has injured others. I am beginning to learn how to give myself the compassion needed to allow my fearful thoughts and emotions to dissolve. I am no longer blaming people and circumstances for my own suffering. Now I hope to stop blaming myself and find an entirely new option.
	
When I was four years old I watched the movie Jaws. The ocean, I understood, is where fear resides. But that’s not really how it is. Fear resides in my own mind. The ocean is just the ocean and sharks are just sharks. I understand now that there is some remote possibility that if I go into the ocean that a shark might eat me. But if I allow my fear to dictate where I go and what I do, then I may never go into the ocean and play in the waves.
	When my children were young, we took them to the ocean for kayaking. I and my oldest daughter, Anna, became seasick. Our guide, Max, suggested that we jump in the cool water to relieve our nausea. I felt sure that as soon as I jumped in the ocean that I would be eaten by a shark, but I jumped in anyway, and so did my daughter. I did not want to pass on my fear to her.
	
	This weekend I went on a solo backpacking adventure in the Pecos Wilderness of New Mexico. As I was signing the trail register, I noticed someone had written a warning about cougars. “Stay together!” they wrote. I was by myself. There was no one to stay together with. As I set out on the trail I felt like prey, scanning the mountain above and below for movements of large cats.
	After seven exhausting hours on the trail, I pitched my tent, made myself dinner, and built a large fire. The crackling warmth of the flames made me feel safe and secure. It was the first time I had ever been in the wilderness all by myself. I went to sleep watching the campfire from the tent. Two hours later I awoke suddenly. The fire was out. It was dark. There was a mysterious clicking sound close to my camp. I was terrified.
	I lay there for some time, hoping that I could fall back to sleep, but I could not. I finally sat up in the tent as straight as I could. I listened to the strange clicking sound. A light rain began to fall on my small tent. The sound of the raindrops hitting the tent just inches from my head was unpleasant. I startled with fear when the logs in the fire shifted suddenly. My brain started inventing other reasons to be fearful, but panic was not an option. I was miles from anyone or anywhere. No cell reception. No help. no comfort.
	I recalled my meditative practice and then I calmly prayed. I focused my attention on my breath. I realized that no matter how unpleasant the sound of the rain was, I could not change that. I accepted that I was vulnerable and alone in a dark forest with only a thin layer of fabric between me and the wild. I had no control over any of this. The only thing I had any chance to control was my response to fear.
	Eventually the rain stopped. I recalled something I had read earlier that day from Pema Chodron. She had encouraged me to move toward my difficulties instead of trying to push them away. I wondered, “What if I get out of this tent and just sit outside in the dark?” I allowed myself some room to make friends with this idea. Part of me was resistant, but another part of me thought that that might be okay. Minutes passed. As I opened the tent, the sound of the zipper seemed to echo throughout the canyon. I found my sandals and then I sat again, waiting for the courage to go out into the dark, closer to whatever threats, real or imagined, might await me. As I finally emerged I could see the outlines of large rocks and trees. I sat on a large stone by the extinguished campfire. I could still feel some warmth emanating from the darkened coals.
	As I stared into the dark forest, I allowed myself to become aware of my breathing. Thoughts came and went. I imagined a sudden piercing howl. I imagined that Golem was creeping around on the rocks just outside my camp. I imagined that a large cougar was stalking me, skulking down the mountain in preparation to attack. But, every time a thought came I allowed myself to return to the stillness that was all around me. I told myself, “none of these imaginations are actually happening.” What was happening in my surroundings? Stillness. The mysterious clicking had stopped. The rain had ceased. There was absolutely no wind. I was in the middle of a profoundly still forest. Exquisite stillness.
	When I allowed the stillness between my thoughts to align with the stillness of the Pecos Wilderness there was harmony. Stillness within. Stillness without. As the fears came I understood that I had a choice. I could allow my mind to entertain fear, but then I would be missing what was really going on. So I just kept coming back to the stillness, and when I did that, there was no fear. I had no control over what might happen in the next moment. But that was really none of my business. I was much more concerned with what was actually happening, this Exquisite Stillness. I was eventually able to return to my sleeping bag.  I slept until the sun rose.

	Hiking down the mountain is often much easier than hiking up. Me and my son Joseph have hiked together a number of times. He likes to consider all that potential energy that we store up in our bodies as we are hiking up the mountain which is now being converted into kinetic energy as we make our way down. 
	I made my way down the mountain to my little blue Toyota with a cracked windshield. Still sensing the release of this kinetic energy as I drove home from my solo backpack adventure, I called my father. I wanted to talk to him about how I had felt in my role as potential cougar food, because I knew he would understand. He loves to think about the terrors of being eaten alive. Our conversation was the impetus for the words you are now reading.
	I have lived for most of my adult life powerless to control how I respond to fear. My fears were so deeply imbedded that I didn’t even understand that they were fears. I participated in psychotherapy; I joined churches; I prayed; I had demons cast out of me; I went to AA meetings; I exercised; I studied; I tried to be a loving person; I became an atheist; I meditated; I became a feminist; I had intelligent and caring friends; I listened to my wife; I went back to school and got a better job; I wrote songs; I performed music; I got standing ovations; I did many things to try to fix myself, but fear continued to dictate how I perceived the world. Even when the forest was still, my brain was inventing fears.
	I want to be careful here that I don’t sound like I have the answer, but if I am truthful, I feel like I do. I will try to explain. Today the answer for me is acceptance of what-is, and confidence that a power greater than myself is able and willing to restore me to sanity. When I am in the dark forest all by myself and my brain is inventing a half dozen reasons that I should be scared, I now know that I am not obligated to follow these story lines. If I am willing to look inward toward this Exquisite Stillness, I can experience a profound shift in my perception, a miracle of perception change. Today I am willing to experience a deeper Reality than I was able to conjure for myself. If I allow it, this deeper Reality will flow from within me. A well has been unstopped that I had allowed to be clogged by all of my fearful suffering.
	All of those things I said about my father, those things are true about me too. And if that is the reality that I choose, I render myself powerless, bound to my fearful story lines. 
No matter how hard I tried, I could not think myself out of my inherited mental illness. But today something has changed. Today I have a reprieve from my insanity. Today I am focused on this Exquisite Stillness.

…And so the first story of the mountain was told by the middle-aged-man, the story of the Exquisite stillness. This was the story of a man who had been trying to make peace with his father for a lifetime, and the story of a father who had also been trying to make peace with his son.

And this is how the writer, the one who was sitting at his mother’s home in Oklahoma City, came to witness again the long journey up the mountain that he had been making over the previous year. For it had been three-hundred-and-sixty-seven days since he had driven his little blue toyota, the one with the severely cracked windshield, to the Emergency room instead of going to work on that December day. It was just over a year since the middle-aged-man found himself walking the circular hallway of the psychiatric unit in Roswell where he had received the prayer of restoration from the One called Clinton (that Universal Crossroads), the gay black man who had interceded on behalf of the man, the man who had just had another cavity search, the one who had just prayed, “God, please help me.”

But there is still another mountain story to be told, dear reader. And the dying author is very much looking forward to the possibility of sharing that story with you. The story of how a man flew up a mountain with a feather and was rocked in the arms of a an evergreen.

Day 11
8:03 AM

And so it came to pass that the writer prepared for his journey to Bentonville, where he was to meet with his two new publisher friends. He sat in the red and white checkered chair by the large windows in his mother’s living area adjacent to her kitchen. His mother emptied the dishwasher. His eldest studied her part for her lead role in a French comedic opera. His youngest was mischievously interacting with the world as usual. His son slept. The man put his arm around his son-in-law.
 
Leaving early would give him a chance to visit the art museum that his mother had been recommending to him for years, his mother, the Universal Glow. “Am I idealizing my mother? Maybe, thought the wildly talented writer. And also, maybe I can meet with my old friend Van Gogh today, that one who struggled in this world, that one who tried so desperately in his relationship with light to make peace with this swirling cosmos, that one who painted the starry starry night. Our Dear Vincent who truly did paint the divinity that he witnessed. The one who passed down to his brothers and sister the vision of seeing life that way.

It was with deep trust and affection for all that-is that the writer, somewhat hesitantly left his joyful family that morning. He left them in Love. He left them with thanksgiving in his heart.

Amen and Selah.

The windshield of the man’s little blue Toyota was iced over. He needed a tool. He started the engine, turned the heat on high defrost, and picked up Dickey Lee Erwin’s CD case from the middle console. CD cases can make good ice scrapers when its not too cold and the ice is not too thick. The process did not take all that long. Soon he was back in the driver’s seat.

He put the car in drive. He used the neighbors driveway for a turn-around. As he passed house after house in his mother’s comfortable neighborhood, he was reminded of the mandolin that he’d left behind. His guitar was with him, but his mandolin was not. As a prayer of willingness to listen, he turned the car around. He parked the blue Toyota in his mother’s driveway, went inside, grabbed the mandolin, said another short goodbye to his family, then passed over the threshold of his mother’s home again. 

He found the Rucker Brother’s CD, the album that he had written and produced with his old friend from music school. The album spoke to him of hope, his friends last name, Matthew Hope. He praised the Spirit of Music which chose to flow so freely through his friends hands in the album they had created together. His friend was truly a conduit, some might say a genius. 

The album played out. The middle-aged-man called his friend Matt. No answer. Voicemail not setup. The man texted his friend Chris with an invitation to call him. No response. The man continued the journey on his own, giving praise for the sense of purpose that he felt in his being, giving thanks for the clarity in his mind and soul, giving thanks for death and the resurrection that he witnessed in his own being,  He picked up his phone and accessed its audio-recording app. He raised the phone to his lips.

The traveller spoke the following words that day as he scooted down the Oklahoma turnpikes in his little blue toyota, stopping to pay the tolls along the way.
 
The man dictated his words into his telephone in order to be with you like this now, dear reader. Look, dear reader, do you see him there now? What is it that he is holding? I believe he is praying for us…“I pray that God would keep us rooted, right here in this moment, the eternal now, moving at the speed of life. Gratefulness in these hearts. Thanksgiving on these lips. Peace. Peace. Peace to you, my dear reader.”


Maybe following Life is like foraging as a hunter gatherer—seeing the nuts and the berries, following the herd of animals as they migrate. Maybe it’s more like that than obedience to military General. 

The writer, the middle aged man, the dying author, this traveller, held his phone close to his face and spoke into the audio recording app. He reveled in the idea of his words being captured in real time as he drove down the road. He passed a sign for Braums, a fast food restaurant. He had worked there as a soda jerk in his twenties. It was his first job after he and his wife had sold all their possessions and poured those drinks out in the winter wheat field. 

The traveller tapped his brakes, dropping the little blue toyota out of cruise control in order to stay with the flow of traffic. “I’m just moving at the speed of Life,” he happily reminded himself out loud. He was on interstate 244 East heading toward downtown Tulsa.  

Tulsa OK, the city in which a young man and his wife spent their first married evening. The traveler took the Joplin exit where the road forked. He laughed in recognition of the binary. Yet, in that moment his forward motion did not cease, for he did not wish to cause a traffic accident. His GPS told him that he had sixty five more miles to go until the next fork in the road.

He passed the Bartlesville exit. Bartlesville, that town where one of his old friends was from. Kevin, the gifted guitar player. Years ago, a young man and his friend Kevin were musical partners. Kevin was the one who had a heavy correction for the hitchhiker sitting there at Denny’s waiting for a ride. 

Kevin, the man who had spent six years in prison--six soul crushing years in a federal prison. How does a man process that kind of suffering? How does one learn to survive in places like that? Where one can hardly ever feel safe? Where one cannot masturbate in private? 

“What the fuck are you doing?! You’re not some kind of fucking hobo. I mean, what the fuck are you doing out there on the goddamn highway? You’ve got responsibilities! You need to take care of your business.” 

This was how Kevin’s voice arrived in the middle-aged-man’s ears as he sat at Denny’s, his hitchhike coming to a conclusion. With as calm a voice as he could maintain, the middle-aged-man finally said, “with love and respect, Kevin, I’m going to hang up the phone now.” He had not heard from Kevin since.

The traveller took a moment to make his heart right toward his old friend. He took a moment to allow this miracle to happen in his own heart as he drove through downtown Tulsa, the place where he and his wife had spent their first married night. 

Today, the man felt his own company, and it was good company. And the man felt the company of you, dear reader, as he drove on those elevated interstates. “fifty eight miles to the next fork in the road,” a large green sign informed the traveller. 

He marvelled at the joy that was in his heart—this pure, simple, clean, light-hearted experience. He knew that if a word search was done on this manuscript, that the word “experience” would certainly be the most used. “That’s appropriate,” thought the writer, “considering where I came from. Considering that album made by the twenty-something year old Jimi Hendrix, who’d asked a fourteen year old boy that question, ‘Are You Experienced.’” At eighty miles per hour, the traveller laughed out loud. That fourteen year old boy took that question into his heart. “Are you Experienced?” That fourteen year old boy took that album into his heart. He took that creation into his heart. Noel Redding on Bass. Mitch Mitchell on Drums. Yes, that music captured that fourteen year old boys heart. It was the boys father who had made that introduction.

With fifty-four miles before the next fork, he breathed in the joy of life. He breathed in all of the connection that he was sensing. He rested in the weave of life. The first time he took the medicine, that’s what he had seen. He saw the fabric of being. He saw the fabric of community. And he saw himself supported in that fabric. Yes! It was more than sight, more than seeing. With the help of the medicine, the middle-aged-man was able to experience a felt sense of being supported.

It was then that the traveller was able to acknowledge fear in his own heart. “There it is. Right there,” he said. “Fear, I acknowledge you.” He felt the fear in his chest. “Fifty one miles to the next fork,” he announced. 


Yes. He felt the fear in his heart—a fear that maybe he’d missed it. Fear that maybe he hadn’t worked hard enough. Fear that he should be at work right now in the physical therapy clinic. He braked abruptly, almost passing a state trooper doing sixty-five in a fifty-five.

The traveller remembered that a feeling-of-fear is not the same as a story-inspired-by fear.

He passed a billboard for a gentleman’s club called “Sensations.”

Bright orange pylons and arrows pointed in many directions. His travel slowed to 55.

“Hey, lookie there!” he laughed out loud, “Rick Springfield is scheduled to play at the Hard Rock Café, casino, and Resort”

He looked down at his gas gauge--less than a quarter of a tank. He opened his heart to his environment. A red GMC pickup truck passed on the left. The writer thought of those trackers who see the world through the animals’ eyes. He checked his GPS--sixteen miles to the Stone Creek gas station in Inola, Oklahoma. His GPS indicated his arrival time in Bentonville to be around noon, which would give him an hour to see if he could find Mr. Van Gogh at the art museum.

Editor’s note:
Is it critical of me to ask these questions: “What is the point of all this? Am I getting to know a character? Am I endeared to this character? Do I have compassion for this character? Does this character deserve compassion? Is he likable? Do you like this person that you see? Is his voice friendly? Whose voice? Critical voice? 

And so the road seemed to open up wide in front of him as he approached the horizon over a hill. Tulsa disappeared in his rearview mirror—forty two miles to the next fork in the road.

As the left lane closed down for construction up ahead, the traffic lined up in single file. The man came to a fork in the road of his own—that place all writers encounter when they must choose between one story or the other. 

Left Fork: The writer had a vision of a chimpanzee walking on two legs. In his arms and hands, the chimp holds as many oranges as he can carry. Can you see the chimp, dear reader? Can you see him grasping on to all the food he can possibly carry with his hands-- and even with his feet? His ancestors came down from the trees so that we could carry all these oranges today! This is one of the benefits that comes with bipedal motion. “Yay! Now we can carry food, and babies, and tools! This is gonna be awesome!”

Right Fork: The writers second choice in the binary involved a recount of the only honeymoon that he’d ever been on. Ahhh….path of the broken promises. 

The writer was pleased as he waited at the fork in the road. He paused at the binary. He chose neither. He waited in the rise and fall of his own chest.

Both lanes opened up again. Then the orange signs reappeared and the road narrowed to a single lane. The gas station approached, less than a mile ahead. Massive power lines overhead. He merged into the right turning lane. He pulled into the Sinclair station. There was the green dinosaur. His father let him ride the green Sinclair dinosaur when he was very little.

“How closely is this narrator related to the observer?” the writer wondered. He pumped his gas and he went inside. “The I AM simply observes. The narrator is the one who tells story with language,” he finally answered himself. He stood at the urinal, speaking into his telephone, wondering if someone would walk in on him mid-sentence.

As he made his way through the gas station and out the door, he realized his hunger. He had not eaten that morning except for a few candied pecans from his mother’s kitchen island--those lovely pecans, so attractively offering themselves. 

He unlocked the car remotely. His car beeped. He pulled on the silver handle. He sat, opening the tupperware he’d packed. His mother’s fridge and pantry had much to offer him that morning. He munched on some of his aunt Lindi’s famous cheese log. He savored a couple of pieces of chicken that his mother prepared the night before. He didn’t want to be too full. He was scheduled to have a meal with Dave and Eileen. He dipped a frito in a soft avocado. He tasted a peanut butter cookie. One final bite of chicken. Then he started his car and navigated out of the parking lot. He turned right twice, and merged onto the Interstate.

“You’re packing too much food,” his youngest daughter’s voice rang in his ears. 

“I believe you are correct,” he said out-loud as he completed his merge into the right lane. 

The writer was looking for dialogue opportunities. “What else is it that I’m trying to develop in my writing?” he asked himself. “Oh yes, I want to allow the reader to experience the story with their senses. Also, with descriptive details, I’m creating environment in which dialogue can occur. I’m choosing the Universal message as I allow the dialogue to form. It’s not at all important that I remember exactly what was said. It’s mostly important that I allow Life to show me the Universal Message in the conversation.” 

“Remember, we are writing a novel here, a work of fiction, a novel very loosely based on the life of one  middle-aged-man,” a writing coach had suddenly shown to offer a little help in the creative process.

“Oh, Universe!” proclaimed the man, “You really are out to help me?” the writer proclaimed joyfully.

The middle-aged-man often found himself wondering what other people remembered about certain conversations they’d had. He’d discovered that, often times, the conversations that turned out to be so important to him, his loved ones could not remember. Dialogue, the reconstructed memory of a conversation. 

He was curious about first memories too. As a father raising his children, he wondered which moments in the lives of his children would be formative, so he questioned them as they were growing up. He questioned his children about their memories. He was curious about how they remembered life. Just that morning, in fact, one of his children asked the man about a memory. 

“Do you remember when your brother was born, Dad?” asked his son. 
“Yes, I do,” he said. “Let me show you how I remember it.”
The father lowered himself to his knees in front of the fireplace, next to the snake-skin-pattern chair--the floor-rug textured with yellows and greys. 

“This is how I remember my brother being born,” he said.

The man held an imaginary car in his right hand. It was an orange volkswagen Beetle that zoomed forward when you pulled it back. He pulled the car back on the thick carpet and then he watched as it rolled forward on the hospital lobby floor. When the car came to a stop, the little boy retrieved it joyfully and repeated the process.

The middle-aged-man’s three children watched intently as the little boy played with his new toy that his parents bought to keep him occupied as he waited for his little brother to be born.

As the little boy stood up he was once again a man, the father of the children who were, for that brief moment, witness to a memory. 

“And that’s what I remember about my brother being born,” he said to them. “I don’t have a memory of a baby boy for some reason, but I do remember waiting in the hospital lobby, playing intently with that little car,” he said. “But, you know what?” he questioned rhetorically. “I am not for sure that the car was self-powered like I said it was. I feel pretty certain that it was an orange car, but I’m not exactly sure that it was a volkswagen Beetle. That event happened over forty one years ago. I was four years old. I have played and replayed that memory for myself many times throughout my life. I can’t really be certain of much surrounding that memory, but that’s the story I just told surrounding the birth of my brother. That is the evolving memory that came to signify his birth in my mind.”

Thirty four miles to the next fork in the road…





Editor’s Note: This feels sad to me in this moment, that the middle-aged-man has no memory of his brother being born. The editor searched his own heart for his brother’s memory. “How much of my memory was formed by pictures? What was it like to remember before their were pictures? Our pictures form so much of what we remember. Was there a picture of me playing on the floor with the orange car? Is that why I had a memory?” the editor questioned himself.



The narrator. The observer. .
The one who tells the stories. The one who simply is

This is right where his practice started with Penny. Penny, the one who’d asked the man this question: “What percentage of the voices in your head are your father’s?” Although it had been many years since she’d asked him that question, he still remembered his answer and how quickly it had come to him. 

“Eighty five percent,” said the man in Penny’s office. He sat on the couch, hugging a large throw-pillow.

He immediately reflected on his answer, suspicious of the speed with which he’d answered. It did seem like a reasonable number. So he said it once again, “eighty-five percent.”

The man who sat in Penny’s office was one of the top-five-self-bludgeoners-that-she’d-ever met. This is the man who estimated that eighty-five-percent of the voices that spoke to him were the critical voice of his father. Before meeting Penny, the man was unaware of the damage that he’d been heaping upon himself.

“Andy Dufresne,” said Penny, “Do you know Andy Dufresnes?”

“Yes,” said the man, “He’s the guy in Shawshank Redemption.”

“Andy Dufresne,” Penny repeated the name. “The man who crawled through a river of shit and came out clean on the other side. This isn’t gonna be easy,” she said, “and it’s not gonna happen fast, but you’re going to come out clean on the other side.”

“How many times,” wondered the traveller, “how many times since then have I thought that I have completed my crawl through that shit-tube? How many times?” 

He knew the answer did not matter. He knew that there was no use worrying about it. The number of times was literally uncountable. It would be impossible for the man to remember every recovery of his life. He knew that all he had was this moment. All he had was today. 

He felt sure that the best thing he could do for himself now was to keep making friends—always making friends with himself first and then allowing that friendship to spread out and touch others. 

He would receive the friendship that was being offered to him today. No expectations. No demands on life. The AA literature had suggested to him the possibility of, “Living life on life’s terms.” Or, as the middle-aged-man had been saying to himself recently, “I let go, let life do the living.”

The traveller encountered a fork in the road that he’d not expected. He went left and it was the wrong choice. Tara, his GPS was speaking up now, in her greatest effort to correct his error. The traveller now found himself on unexpected detour on a two-lane highway.

 After he allowed his initial sense of, “Oh shit! I took a wrong turn,” to pass, the traveller settled in to the great What-Is. The detour curved through a blackjack oak forest. The turns were tight and the line was doubled in the middle of the road. Yellow caution signs indicated S-turns ahead. Rural homes dotted the sides of the highway. The hills and curves and double- lines persisted. 

Horses were out to winter pasture. The oaks stood tall and proud now by a billboard for a community bible church. Black angus cattle munched on golden grasses. A tin barn rusted to death where it stood. An american flag waved gently in the breeze. And another flag. And another still. 

He was informed by a passing sign that he was on scenic-route 412. He passed a church parking lot full of cars. He carefully observed a two-way stop as his GPS faithfully was faithfully recalculating his way back to the main higway. Nw in a neighborhood, he topped a steep hill. There was a writer in him now, a hunter gatherer, looking for tasty morsels of americana.

“How will this read?” he wondered.

As the on-ramp found him there were thirteen miles to the next predicted fork in the road. The writer felt quite certain that if he had not been narrating so vigorously that he would have chosen the correct fork earlier and could have avoided that detour. 

“That’s the nature of the narrator.” The writer instructed himself, “The narrator is chronically in the past, and when the vehicle is moving at seventy-five miles an hour, and the narrator is at the forefront of the consciousness you’re probably gonna miss your turn. “So be it,” he thought, “at least there’s a point to all this chatter. At least there’s some kind of lesson.” He felt satisfied with that.

But that younger version of himself, the one who sat on Penny’s couch, had never ever heard of the Observer or the Narrator. Do you recall, dear reader, the one whom I am speaking of? There he sits, on Penny’s couch. Do you see him? Yes, dear reader, he is hugging a pillow and coming to terms with himself. The one who we were with before we took that wrong turn back there, dear reader. 

He had surely met the observer many times--many times had he rested in those arms of the the great I Am. He had known that sincerity. He had lived in that authenticity. He had tasted this reality many times in his life, just as we all have, dear reader. 

It was just that he was not aware of that critical voice. It was not until that day—the day that he sat on Penny’s couch, that he became aware of a critical voice in his own head. Before that, the critical eighty-five percent was invisible. Or rather, it hid in plain sight. 

Eighty five percent of his self-talk was the echoing voice of a critical father. And he had been unaware. He was not obligated to listen to the critical voice. But he did. And this is how he’d earned the title of one-of-the-to-five-self-bludgeoners-that-Penny-Sanders-had-ever-met.

Hazards blinking, An SUV merged from the shoulder into the right lane, hazards. The traveler signalled and gave way to the one who was merging.

As he passed the merging SUV the writer recalled a book that he’d read about the Anneagram. He had taken a personality test that helped him to identify his own clinging ego. The book suggested that he write an autobiography in third person, but he’d ignored that advice and he pushed on in the first-person instead. He spent many hours in the reconstruction and the owning of a story that he had already told himself a hundred-thousand times—a story of pain and suffering told in the first person which reclaimed over and over again all of the trauma that he’d experienced.

Only three miles now to the predicted fork in the road. The GPS was saying “yes, take a left this time, my friend.” A toll plaza approached.

So Penny gave the man a great gift. He came back time and time again to sit and face that unconquerable wall of despair, facing the trauma that had been locked in his body for years, as Penny offered him her “unconditional positive regard.” Praise be to Penny. Praise be to Carl Rogers.

“This is not going to be easy,” Penny said, “And it’s not going to happen fast. But you will come out clean on the other side.” 

Today, this traveler believed Penny. Once again, he believed her. For he had today: this hope, this reflection, this presence. Yes, dear reader, today he celebrated his story of recovery as he felt himself on a pilgrimage to visit his old friend Vincent Van Gogh, and his newer friends, Dave and Eileen.

He was just an hour from Bentonville now, and he thought that he might give the Observer some time to navigate. For some time he breathed in. He breathed out. He oriented himself to his passing surroundings.

The middle aged man wondered about the adobe house and the college-cow-town. He wondered about Eli, and how he was. It seemed like ages ago that he’d stood facing North into the great mirror of the kitchen. He crossed the border. Arkansas had found him!

The traveller sang gently, “Moving at the speed of life. .Moving at the speed of life. Moving at the speed…moving at the speed…Moving at the speed of life.” 

The traveler, who was moving now at precisely the speed of life, arrived at the Art Museum. It was an American art museum, so, no Van Gogh. But, he did meet Sapphos of Lesbos. He left the museum. He navigated to the Bentonville square. He had lunch with Dave and Eileen. They walked the wooded gravel paths and briefly explored the museum together. The writer brought his hands together. They said their goodbyes. The man got back in his little blue Toyota with the cracked windshiled. Then, he reprogrammed Tara, the GPS, to guide him back his mother’s home in Oklahoma City.

As he drove away from the Bentonville square he pulled into an angled parking space. He quieted the engine. He closed his eyes. He brought his hands together. He asked for direction. He placed his thumbs on his forehead and bowed to his own heart. He asked that he be able to let go of his own schedule, that he would be empowered to follow the signs of life like a hunter-gatherer, that he could see through the eyes of the animal that he followed. 

As he began his drive home, he waited on a traffic light at Gene George and Sunset. He turned West on Sunset Avenue. He waited for the miracle, that which he could not do for himself.

He left Bentonville on I-49. Life presented to him two small flocks of geese and one large circling of vultures. He recalled his lesson in ACIM from the night before. It had something to do with knowing that what we see is not…real? “Is that what Helen was saying?” wondered the traveler. “Or perhaps she was saying that so much of what we think we see is really our past informing our present.What might happen if I truly became aware when the past is dictating to me my interpretation of the present moment? What would it be like to live in a reality in which I am truly aware of the present?” The traveller acknowledged that he had felt a dark meaning in the flock of vultures.  “Maybe I do not know the meaning of those birds,” he said.

As he was leaving Bentonville, he saw a sign in the yard of a home. “Psychic Readings,” said the sign. The traveler stopped. He rang the doorbell. No one answered. He prayed that his willingness to stop would be received as a prayer. 

It would take a miracle to get him home tonight without consuming caffeine. It would take a miracle to keep him alert and awake. He was not a good night-time driver. And with his sleep being erratic recently, he was concerned that he might have some kind of physiological crash on his way home. He was fearful of that undeniable force that so often pushed his eyelids down as he drove at night.

The billboard at Freedom Fellowship read “Peace, be still. Jesus.”

He had been less than still in his interaction with Dave and Eileen (Eileen and Dave). He had allowed himself to become what he called, “playful.” He allowed himself to say and do things that he would not have said or done around normal people. The writer questioned himself about what he meant when he said “normal people.”

“What was that that? Who was that playful person that I just allowed myself to be with Dave and Eileen? Was that the manic person that scares people away? The manic person that the mother of my children does not want to be friends with? And what was it about Dave and Eileen that made me feel free to be that?” the writer asked himself. “And was it appropriate that I allowed myself to be that?” he wondered still.

ACIM was directing him to let go of the meaning that he assigned to things. This aligned with his teachings from Pema, just allowing things to be. It seemed the wisest course to allow the time that he’d just spent with Eileen and Dave to just be. He was not obligated to derive any meaning or significance from the time that they’d spent together. He was not obligated to force any meaning on the circling vulures. “I remained grateful and buoyant during my meeting with them,” the traveler encouraged himself. “I remained open and playful. I listened. I shared. I enjoyed myself in their company.” As the sun began to set, the traveler did his best to make peace with himsef and with his memory Bentnville, and of Dave and Eileen.

It was five in the afternoon. All the cars headlights were shining. He drovepast a rock face that highway workers had once carved. He passed a log cabin on stilts. The sun would be down in a few minutes—the landmarks greyed in his sight.

Round green haybales dotted a small circular field. A bridge carried the traveller over the Illinois River. He passed a barn that had been constructed to look like a ship. 

He felt the beginnings of sleepiness in his eyes. “Do I hold too much hope in this writing project?” he asked himself. “Why do I feel so insecure righ now?” His two and a half hour meeting with the publishers had depleted him emotionally. There was no way around the hope that he felt in his desire to become a writer. 

A great exhaustion came over the traveller. He struggled to stay awake.

“Enter the gates with thanksgiving. Ener the courts with praise. I give you thanks, Oh God for the gasoline that is in my tank. I give you thanks that this vehicle has made it this far, to this spot in the road.” The traveller frantically munched on snacks. Can you see hime there, dear reader? Do you see this exhausted man trying to get home. His eyelids want to close right now. “I give thanks for the pink hue of the clouds against a blue but greying sky. I give thanks for the food that’s going into my body right now.” He filled his body with sugars hoping to get a little lift. “I give thanks that I have options today. I can get a caffeine drink if it’s an emergency. I can also beseech God for a miracle.” He felt desperate. That’s honest. He felt desperate. “This is why the mother of my children doesn’t like my manics,” he told himself, “There’s always the crash that comes afterward. And here it comes.”

 I give thanks for the deepening contrast of the pinks and blues in the sky. I give thanks for the depth that I can see as I look at that pink sky. I am process. I give thanks for the process which works through me.”

The traveller sang, “Moving at the speed of life.”

He was pretty sure that he stole that melody from The Prince of Egypt soundtrack.


Day 12
5:32 AM

The next morning, having successfully stayed awake on his trip back from Bentonville, the middle-aged-man sat in the living room with his mother. His mother, the Universal Glow, who sat next to the gas burning fireplace in a snake-skin patterned chair. The middle-aged-man was seated comfortably on the couch—the couch which had served as the man’s bed for the previous three nights. 

The man’s children, his mother’s grandchildren, still slept in the guest rooms just on the other side of the hallway—the hallway which was lined with many pictures of all the family that flowed in and out of the home in which they now sat.

The man loved his mother. He was deeply grateful for her presence in his life. His mother still held him. Over the last year, his mother had done much to help him hold his own form. The mother had done all that she could do to bring her son into the world, and also, to see him be born again. She had not only witnessed his rebirth once, but many times. There were also births that she had not witnessed, those births in which the mother had not been directly involved. 

But, as she sat there in the snake-skin chair, the mother was an audience to her son’s current rebirth. She was close. She also knew that she was only a witness. She watched as her son allowed himself this process. She watched as the son was letting go. She watched, and yes, she was worried, but she knew how to give her son the room he needed to do what he was doing. And the son knew how to allow his mother to be a mother. He knew that she would worry, and he was somehow able to allow her to be just who she was in this process. He was able to feel her worrying for him without allowing himself to be overwhelmed by her worry. And these were the nuances of the push-and-pull in the relationship between the man and his mother—the mother who now sat in the snake-patterned chair in front of the softly hissing gas fireplace.

The son and the mother spoke of the day’s plans. He and his children were scheduled to have lunch with his father at Louie’s on lake Hefner. 

Son-in-law asks to trim the father’s beard?

As the mother and son spoke, the youngest daughter seated herself next to the middle-aged-man on the couch. “Do you remember last night?” she said “I got up to get a drink of water and you were standing over there crying, Dad. I gave you a hug and asked you if you were okay, and you said, ‘somethings happening.’” She continued. “I’m nervous about meeting with Papa,” she said.

She wanted to talk about her grandfather and how she felt about the upcoming meetin. She spoke of her apprehension toward her grandfather. She spoke of a fear that she had in relating to him. 

“Your grandfather suffers deeply,” He said. The middle-aged-man spoke with compassion in his heart. “He was never held in the way that he needed. He was never seen or known. He was physically, emotionally, and verbally abused by a father who had likely had the same done to him.” 

Next, his oldest daughter approached him. They had a similar conversation. The father spoke of generational suffering. He spoke of the healing process. He spoke of needing to be held. He spoke of Andy Dufresne, the man who crawled through a mile of shit and came out clean on the other side. He gave his daughter thanks for the gift of her being. He thanked his daughter for seeing him. He thanked his daughter for listening. He thanked her for her smiles and her hugs. He thanked her from the bottom of his heart and he was glad.

He prayed for the father who they were scheduled to meet. He prayed that the father would have his own awakening experience, and he prayed that their meeting today would play its part in the great awakening of the Father.

Text to father:

Son: Is there any room at the inn for me tonight? I’m trying to birth this Christ Child

Father: First…yes…second what does that mean?

Son: It just means I’m alive. I’m writing a story. I’m witnessing the birth of life in my being every moment. And so, everywhere I go I’m seeing this Universal awareness being born and I am acknowledging that same birth in others

Dad: sounds like the makings for a great new year!! See you soon.. much love your way!

There was a game that the middle-aged-man used to play with his kids. The family affectionately called it the “stay-game.” The game involved the man hugging his children and not allowing them to leave. The hold on the child was released. The child would attempt to get away. As the child attempted to move away, the hug was then reestablished by the father with the words, “Stay. Stay. Don’t go. Stay.” It was a big joke. At least the man hoped that’s what it was. “A joke, right?” the man thought to himself. 

The man hoped that he understood the parody that he was showing to his children. “Guilt is a joke,” was the lesson that the middle-aged-man had tried to convey to his children. The man wondered if he’d taught his children well enough. The man wondered if he had yet to fully take this lesson into his own hear--this lesson that he had attempted to teach to his children.

“Guilt is not a joke,” he said to himself. He wondered why he taught his children that. He hoped it was enough to get some kind of point across to them, the point of a lesson that he may or may not have yet to learn.

And it was in this way that the middle-aged-man and his children left the grandmothers home that day. After lunch the children would head South to visit other grandparents. The man was scheduled to spend the night with his father.




1:31
Day 12

After the meal at Louie’s, the travelling son followed his father home from Lake Hefner. He fell asleep in a bed in his father’s back room. He woke from a dream with a startle. He was unable to remember the dream. The son lay on his back, the weighted blanket, that gift from his oldest daughter, draped over his body. His laptop rested on a folded blanket and a large wooden lap desk that his father told him was his to use while he was there. It may have been his father’s way of encouraging the son in his writing.

The writer’s visit with Eileen and Dave yesterday left him somewhat confused about the purpose of his work. The encounter left the writer wondering, “What is the purpose of all this writing?” Was he writing for the purpose of being published, or was he just writing for his own healing? 

Of course, the writer wanted to be published. Isn’t that what writers want? Isn’t that what the musician always wanted, an audience? (or a bigger audience, or an audience that truly came to listen?). The artist wants to be seen. To be heard. Or at least this artist did. He still wanted to be seen. To be heard. To be acknowledged.

Text from MOM:

Mom: Happy Monday (winking smiley face emoticon)

Son: Hi mom

Mom: hello…how’s your day?

Son: I’m okay. I’m at dad’s house. I just woke up from a nap. The kids are on their way to Caldwell to see Dan and Laronna

Mom: should I expect to see you tonight?

Son: No, not unless some kind of unforeseen emergency arises. I am sowing myself into the soil here. Making myself available for a miracle. Please pray for our peace. I trust the Christ.


The day before, While in Bentonville, Harbor Dave had given the aspiring writer some practical advice. The writer knew it was sound advice, he’d heard it before. One part of the advice went like this: “Show. Don’t tell.” The idea behind this being that the audience needs and wants to smell and taste and touch and hear and see the environment in which their beloved character is immersed. The audience does not want to be told about the character. The audience would rather experience the environment for themselves, they want to be there as the story unfolds,and the best way for this to happen is by allowing them to feel the world through the character’s senses. The writer truly had no way to know if he was even coming close to gifting his audience with this kind of experience. 

The next bit of advice from Harbor Dave was also simple, at least in concept: “Dialogue,” he said, “dialogue.”

Harbor Dave continued,“The audience wants to listen to your character interact with others,” Dave explained to the inexperienced writer as their feet crunched on the graveled paths in Bentonville. “They don’t care whether you can remember exactly how the conversation took place, but they want to know what it sounds like when your character speaks out loud. The audience wants to hear your character. The audience wants to hear the Universal voice in the dialogue between your Universal characters. Your audience wants to observe the characters reactions as they interact with one another. And they want to feel something coming out of that. Your audience wants to be reminded of what they already know, the truths that they know but that they have forgotten, those truths that are buried down so deep that they haven’t been in touch with them recently--or those truths that have yet to surface, that the listener is ripe to hear. That’s why the reader reads--to be reminded of these sacred things. The audience wants to know more about what your character is thinking and saying,” Dave reiterated.

The writer considered, for a long moment, Harbor Dave message. “I’ll have to come back to that,” he said, “thanks Dave.”

“Eileen,” said the writer as he shifted his attention from Dave, “What was it that you were telling me yesterday? What was the Universal Eileen saying? What is it that I heard from her as we met there on the Bentonville square?” What was the Universal Eileen saying, dear reader, as we walked those gravel trails?

“Look sweetie,” said the Universal Eileen, “I just want you to know that everything is okay. It’s okay to get naked. Everything that happens in the shower is beautiful! You just keep walking away and you just keep walking forward/toward/inward/onward. Keep taking those deep breaths--those breaths that give you an unusually large rib cage that kind of sticks out like your kids were pointing out to you. I just want you to know that I DO see you. You are seen by the Universal. Listen, you ARE safe. The Universe IS out to help you, and you are on a beautiful path of discovery that I just want to bless right now. Waking up is a process, my dear, and you are right where you need to be in the middle of all that. I know how to play just like you do, believe me, my friend. There is nothing you can do that’s gonna freak me out, because I have been there. I have been where you are. I have run around my house for days without any clothes on and my kids came over and had to make peace with that. The ambulance came to my house and I had to go away for a little bit and I had the honor of making peace with that too. I am familiar with medical leave, my dear. So, there’s not a bit of judgment about that coming from this direction. You are on the path, because everything IS the path. Be patient as you watch this all unfold. Know that you are held. Know that you are loved. Know that you are supported. And most of all….most of all…remember that none of what’s happening right now is nearly as important as you think it is, and it doesn’t mean what you think it does! Money? Do you remember what Rumi said the other day? Remember sweetie, it was on page 72. This is what he said:

The copper of your being
Has already been transmuted to gold
By Moses Alchemy, and yet you fumble
In a moneybag for loose coins
			Rumi (translation Barks)

You’re worried about money? Sweetie, don’t worry about money. I know people who lived out of their cars for a while, and that was exactly what they needed. It brought them into a deeper experience. So you just don’t worry about what you are going to eat. And you don’t worry about what you are going to wear. Look at the monkey grass in your dad’s backyard and how it’s nice and green and healthy in the middle of winter. You see those plastic ducks in your dad’s cement pond? Well you are buoyant, just like those ducks floating on that water, my dear. You get to stop fumbling through your moneybag for loose coins. I give you permission to be this, I give you permission to be the you that you have always known you were. Matthew 5. Am I right, sweetie? Didn’t you always admire that about the one that you so affectionatley call Jesus for all those years?  Remember Dear, you are not obligated to take all of this too seriously. And believe me, that’s a gift that this world needs very dearly. As you allow the matrix to crumble around you, you are bringing this gift into the world. You remember that man outside the bus station when you were younger? The one in whom I showed you freedom? Do you remember who you went home and shared that with? You are not obligated to make your ex-wife happy, and although you may be legally obligated to provide child support…well, let’s just say that you can’t get blood out of a turnip, my dear. And if your ex-wife (or rather, that person that your ex-wife thinks she is) is hell-bent on squeezing the shit out of turnips, then so be it. But, let me just say that you also can’t get shit out of a turnip. So, in all reality, she’s just gonna be shit out of luck, and that’s gonna be exactly what she needs. She can squeeze turnips from here to your grandma’s house, but she can’t squeeze you. Remember those three big blue books that you were resting your laptop on at the adobe house? What does the course say, dear? What does it say? Do you remember? “Nothing that is real can be threatened. Nothing that is unreal exists.” So, if you are feeling threatened, what do you do? You just give thanks that what is false in you is passing away. Your prayers are being answered. Isn’t that the prayer that you’ve been praying for a year now. How long have you been praying that third step prayer? Do you remember what it says? Of course you do. “Relieve me of the bondage of self, that I may better do your will.” Well, this is one way in which that prayer is being answered. You are not obligated to take all this too seriously. And, you really don’ have to worry. And…listen to me, dear… YOU DON’T HAVE TO GO BACK TO LIVING A MISERABLE LIE. Misery is completely optional here. You aren’t on some kind of vacation right now that must come to an end. You are answering a legitimate calling on your life, and it was true when the Christ said that you are not obligated to worry. So, don’t worry. And lastly, why don’t you just go ahead and put a little book mark right here, because you may need to come back and read this message. This is the new message from the Source. The new message from the Universal which comes from the same source which Eileen’s first email came from. There may be some more I want to say here as you read through it again. 

The meeting with Eileen and Dave (Dave and Eileen) started like this:

Yes. The aspiring writer stood on the square in Bentonville. The air was cold. The sky was grey. Can you see him, dear reader? He is reaching into the pocket of his olive jacket. Yes, dear reader, he is taking a picture. The middle aged man took a landscape shot of Sam-Walton’s first business, the mother of all Wal-Marts. Sam’s old Ford Pickup sat there, just across the street, enshrined for a capitalist’s eternity in front of his five and dime, where all the magic that we experience today as we cross the Wal-Mart’s threshold began. As the writer stood, immersed in his screen, attempting to send this picture to a friend with whom he’d once visited this very sight, he felt warm hands wrapping around his waist from behind.

“Oh my goodness!” he proclaimed, before even turning around to see who it was, “It’s so good to see you!” he said as his eyes met Eileen’s.

Eileen smiled. They hugged. And over Eileen’s shoulder he saw Dave, just fifteen feet away! They had appeared! Harbor Dave and the Universal Eileen. 

Dave welcomed the middle-aged-man to Bentonville with a smile. They exchanged embraces and polite greetings.

“I didn’t realize it would be so cold,” said Dave. “I kind of hoped we’d be able to sit out here on the square, but it’s a bit too much for that, I think. Are you hungry?”

“Yes, I am,” said the man in the olive jacket, “I haven’t had much to eat today.”

“Okay,” said Dave, “there’s a diner just over here. Lunch is on us. My son is feeding us all the time. He’s a chef, a real foodie. But I still think I could eat.”

Dave lead the three of them along the West end of the square until they came to a café on the corner called the Pressroom. The sounds of the restaurant bounced off solid, grey concrete floors. The acidic smell of coffee rose from behind the large island bar. The hostess lead the three of them to a square table on the other side of the coffee and wine bar. Another party of five were already well into their meal, consuming somberly in a little cubby under a multi-colored, textured light fixture.

“How will I keep this up?” thought the writer as he lay on his back at his father’s house. 
“What is the point of all these details?”

“Just keep going,” said Harbor Dave, “You’re creating a space for something to happen here. Just put in the work here. Give yourself to this space. Allow this space to create itself, and then just see what happens.” 

The writer laying on his father’s bed wanted to object. His heart was broken somehow. He was exhausted by his meeting yesterday with Dave and Eileen. He felt somehow disappointed and deflated, like he didn’t get exactly what he’d wanted, even though he’d tried to shore himself up against any such disappointments (He’d rather say that he had surrendered himself to the will of God surrounding the possibility of disappointments, but how could he really know for sure. Something in him was being threatened, that seemed certain). He knew things were never what he thought they were. But, it also seemed that he still had to go through the emotions of it all. He still had to suffer the disappointments even though he knew that things were never what they first appeared to be.

“What did the three talk about? Those three who sat perusing their menues at the pressroom in Bentonville? What was their dialogue that day” wondered the writer. There was a wet spot on the bed where the father’s little chihuaha, Spud, had peed just a couple of hours ago. The wet spot had spread. 

The writer continued his inquiry, the inquiry made by someone who was trying to hear the universal message over the chatter of his own wounded-children. “What was important? What happened there? More importantly, what was being said by the Universe as the three of them came together yesterday? What was the take home?” The writer was trying to see. This was his practice today. To experience the Universal.

Dave, Eileen, and the middle-aged-man found their seats at the café.  The writer placed his black and red computer bag under the table. In the bag was Rumi, and Hafiz, and the book about the Christ that the Christ did not write. Also in the bag, his laptop, this story, the musings that were saving his life. 

He was seated now at a round table now with Dave and Eileen. He was facing the Northwest. The table sat at a forty five degree angle to the lines of the building. Dave was across from him facing South-East. Eileen was to the man’s right, facing South-West (Editor’s note: It was in this way that the writer who recalls this meeting acknowledges the whole of our being as represented by the cardinal directions in the Plotkin model. I think we’re going to need that to get through this next part. Yes. I am still here, dear reader. I am the one who is exploring the past. I flesh-out a little bit of the story hear and there. I am the one who searches for the Universal. I am the one who tries to establish through-line. I am the one who seeks healing as I move through the next chapters).

The son lay in the quiet of the back room of his father’s house. The silver ceiling fan turned over head. Would he be able to hear what Dave and Eileen had to say today? More importantly, would he be able to receive the Universal message that was mixed in with what Dave and Eileen were saying. 

“We’ve all got these egos,” thought the aspiring writer, “we all project and reflect. The question is: Can I experience the Universal Presence?” There was a part of him that had been quite terrified as he listened to Dave and Eileen yesterday—a part of him that felt threatened. “So let’s allow that part of us to dissolve. Or we might allow that part of us to come home. Yes. Let’s allow that threatened one, the prodigal son, to return home. Can I experience the Universal presence? Well, can I? Father?” 

The insecure writer (or a wounded child?) wondered if Dave had shared his writing tips because Dave had noticed some kind of deficiency in the writer’s voice. The word, “marketable,” was mentioned. The aspiring writer—the sensitive artist—the threatened son was not sure how he was feeling about what had transpired yesterday between himself and Dave and Eileen (Eileen and Dave).

“Who is my audience?” thought the man who lay there on the bed in his father’s back room. This morning, as he’d showered at his mother’s house (so much happened in the shower) he understood his audience to be his descendants. As the warm water poured over the man’s body, he hoped and imagined that, in this story, with these words, he was leaving an inheritance for his children’s children. He hoped that these words would let them know that he had been here. He wanted to communicate to them some sign of the great Waking-Up. The man in the shower felt concern for the future generations. He wanted them to be well. He asked that these words be a prayer for the continued awakening of his descendants.

But now, in this moment, his outlook was not as hopeful. He felt the writing project falling apart underneath his feet. He had secretly hoped that he was finding his footing in a new “calling” or a new “profession.” But the meeting with Dave and Eileen at the pressroom seemed deconstructive (well, isn’t that what the death of the false-self so often feels like, dear reader? It is uncomfortable, is it not? when our egos are threatened?) 

He met with Dave and Eileen, two writers who had been writing and marketing for quite some time apparently, for how long he did not know. He sat at the pressroom with the owners of a publishing company, two people who are in the business of creating something that is marketable, and he could hear that in their voices, and he felt threatened (may all that can be threatened return home to the Great I AM). 

He sensed his own inexperience in his relationship with the two of them. He felt that they may have related to him through his own inexperience. He felt exposed. Vulnerable. These are the best words that the aspiring, student-writer had to describe how he felt about the experience with Dave and Eileen yesterday. 

His drive home was brutal as his body was desperately seeking asleep. He felt that he’d lost all his momentum. 

The writer breathed in. He breathed out. He waited at the fork. He breathed at the binary.

“What was the purpose of the Rucker Brothers Album?” he asked himself. The Rucker Brothers album was the creative music project that he self-published with his friend back in 2006. The man continued wondering, “It was never published. Was it ever heard? Did it make a difference in this world? Did it make a difference in my world? It was an excellent album—I can hear the heart of the album. I can hear the excellence of it today, thirteen years later. But, it was not marketable. Or, at least, we did not know how to market it. The Rucker Brothers album was produced and distributed in an ineffective grass roots fashion with seemingly no effect whatsoever on the world (that judgment is probably a little harsh). But, his youngest daughter had asked for a copy this morning. 

“So, it had some kind of effect now, didn’t it? There it is, see? Your descendants being effected by the music,” The writer was very pleased by the way that connection had positively affected his heart.”

Yes! The youngest daughter had asked for a copy of the CD that the middle-aged-man had produced thirteen years ago. It mattered to her! It was a little piece of her history, the sounds on that album. The middle-aged-man and his daughter had listened to the CD earlier that day as they were on their way to lunch. 

“Daddy,” she said.

They were listening to the Rucker Brothers album as they drove down the Kilpatrick Turnpike. They took the elevated curve to the South, which then transported them to the Hefner Parkway.

“Father?” said the writer, “Is this the purpose of the writing? Could this writing that I am doing now be something positive for my children? Is this what the world needs? Does the world need another book? There’s a lot of books already? Are there not enough books, Father?”

“Purposes are hard to nail down from your current perspective,” said the Father, “The world is a complex and interconnected place. Just keep letting me get you pregnant (Oh my God, is it the father that gets the son pregnant? The writer snickered). Just keep birthing the Christ child into the world. It changes you in the very least.”

“Do you remember what the course in miracles was telling you this morning?” said the Father.

“Yes,” said the son, “A course in miracles was telling me that my thoughts were meaningless. It didn’t ask me to believe that my thoughts were meaningless all day long. It just asked me to to consider that my thoughts were meaningless for one minute, five times in a day. 

Then the blue book chimed in, “That’s right, not all the time. Just five times. One minute at a time. Remind yourself that your thoughts are meaningless. Just allow that to be. Can you let all of this go right now? Can you let all this search for meaning go for just a minute right now?” said the course in miracles. “Can you do that right now? For yourself? Can you just allow yourself to be? This practice will help release you from all that you believe.”

“But the TV is loud in the next room and I’m afraid of my dad,” thought the son who lay there under the silver ceiling fan.

“Just for one minute, remind yourself that it’s okay for all your thoughts, your dreams, your desires, your needs to become meaningless. Let’s try. Come on.”

Can I drop these identities for a moment?

There you go…one finger at a time. Just like that. Excellent.

The generative nurturing adult, who was making himself present in the middle-aged-man asked these questions. The middle-aged man rose up from his reclined position facing South. The man transferred his laptop and the wooden lap desk (provided by the man’s father) to a North facing seated position in a brown swivel-chair textured with spirals. The swivel chair was a little wobbly. The wooden lapdesk provided the appropriate support for his writing. 

He looked out onto his father’s back yard where he saw a swimming pool populated with plastic lilies and two orange-billed ducks . This was where his children learned to swim. Beyond the pool, he saw a garden decoration swaying in the wind, a bright yellow plastic sunflower towering above the green monkey grass. 

“Father?”
“Yes, son?”
“What kind of trees are those?”
“let’s ask,” said the father.

After twelve days of writing and relatively little sleep the middle-aged man was tired. He was looking for some kind of resolution. He was searching for the end of this real-time journey. But, he was also not ready to give this journey up prematurely. He felt that he was falling apart. He felt small after his visit with Eileen and Dave. He felt inexperienced. He felt naïve. He wanted to be held. 

His father was in the next room. His father, the man who was never held, the man who could never hold him.

The son clung to his project, a life raft of form in a world that was dying around him. His world was passing away, and he was documenting its passing. When the man dropped his nets to follow the Christ he had actively said “yes” to his own death. Yet, he clung to this project even as he was unable to find a through line. He clung to the project as he showered. He clung to the project in the car. he clung to the project as he ate with his family at Louie’s on lake Hefner. He clung to it as he became willing to let it go. He clung to it because he trusted the dialogue that was being born in himself. He clung because this was the form that Life offered today. He clung because it was his artist’s survival instinct to crawl into the reed basket that floated by.

His father belched in the living room as he watched TV. The gastrointestinal explosion bounced off the hard tile and sheetrock. The son remembered those belches from his childhood. The son was tired. 

His father was in the next room. Here he was at his father’s home where he intended to spend the night. Can you see the son there, dear reader? He has gone to the place that scares him. Do you see him there as he sits on the wobbly swivel chair. Do you see him, dear reader, as he gathers courage?

He remembered something that he read several months ago. “If you want to find out just how awake you really are, go spend a week with your parents. See what happens.” 

“It doesn’t matter if you remember exactly what the people said,” said Dave, “just as long as you get to the heart of the dialogue.” (This was not exactly what Dave had said, but it was exactly the point).

“What dialogue might develop between the middle-aged-man and his father if the writer chose to stop streaming these words. What if the writer took a break? What if the son went to visit his father in the next room? What if the son went in there with the man who belches?” thought the writer.

“Dave and Eileen spoke to me yesterday as if they knew and I did not. Well, that’s how I felt about Dave and Eileen,” said a wounded-child. The son was nervous that the wounded children were out to play. The wounded-children were not exactly safe at dad’s house. 

“Why am I feeling so hurt?” wondered the middle-aged-man. “Is that even a helpful question in this moment?” 

The man felt threatened. The man felt hurt. The man felt small. The man felt scared. The man felt that he was not being heard, and that he would never ever be heard again.

His father belched in the next room. The belch rang off the cold tile and the sheetrock.

“What about the trees in the back yard? That might be a good place to start a dialogue?” said the generative, nurturing Adult. The son was aware that his dad had cut down many trees in order to make that backyard the hospitable place that it now was.

The man in the olive jacket felt that he was out on the edge. This felt like one of the most dangerous places for him to be. His mother knew it. She was worried. He felt it. Were the wounded children afratid that they were going to die here? Were the wounded-children afraid they were going to die? 

There were no mushrooms. There was no Eli. There was no Dan. And the man’s mother was all the way across town. He sat looking out the sliding door in his father’s extra bedroom. He wanted to be rescued--Rescued by these words.  Rescued by meaning. Rescued by purpose. Rescued by an identity. He wanted to feel like he was a writer. But he did not feel that way. He felt like a man with no job who was at his father’s house—the father who belches and belches as he watches TV.

“Are you okay? How can I help?” said the generative nurturing Adult.
“That line of thought will not serve me right now”, said the man.

He got on his knees and prayed the third step prayer which he’d learned from his friends in AA. Then he went out to meet his father for the first time, again.

Day 12
7:05PM

The son ventured out into the living room. He walked to the window. He looked out into the backyard. His father was there with him in the living room. His father was there.

“What kind of trees are those? Out in your backyard,” said the son.

“What’s that?” said the father.

“I’m not quite sure,” said the father, “I think there’s a mix of trees back there.”

From the kitchen, Kathy, his wife proclaimed, “There are some hackberry trees out there. There’s a bunch of hackberries.”

And so it was that the son and father began to talk to one another on that thirtieth day of December in the year of 2019. Once again they had come together. How many times had this happened? How many times had it un-happened with hurt feelings and disappointment?

The son felt apprehension, fear as he sat with his father that day in the living room. The son went low, to the carpet. He began to stretch his hamstrings. Breathing in deeply he extended and flexed his trunk, remembering the kundalini awakening, the rising of the feminine serpent from the root chakra. The son faced the fork in the road many times in the early stages of the conversation. “The tongue of the serpent is forked,” he heard from somewhere in his inner being. He stepped carefully. 

Earlier in the day, there was a sign at Louie’s which advised patrons to “watch your step.” A woman, ignoring the sign, had stumbled down the steps and fallen into the middle-aged-man as he sat eating with his family. He and this woman shared a moment—it was just like in the movies when a woman falls into a man. The middle-aged-man felt a genuine concern for the woman’s welfare. He enjoyed meeting people when they were in a moment of humility, stripped of their sure footing. It felt honest. 

So now, the son watched his step as he related to the father. Gently. Kindly. Compassionately. He watched his step. Do you see him there, dear reader? There he sits stretching his hamstrings on his father’s rug. Do you see him flexing and extending his trunk, dear reader? He is listening to his father. He is being gentle with himself and with his father.

The father had different stories--Stories that the son had never heard before. The father had been digging in a deeper region of his traumatic childhood memories. The father began the retelling of trauma stories.

The father fed him: homemade minestrone soup seasoned with fresh basil and rosemary and packed with fresh carrots, yellow squash, onions, and a dark kidney shaped bean. 

The writer felt was feeling positive about their interaction so far. He wondered if he and his father doing some travelling together. He could open some doors to his father to visit some people that he hadn’t seen in some time. His father had mentioned John Johnson, the father’s ex brother-in-law. The son released the vision of travelling with his father. 

“Let’s write a story together, Dad. Would you like to help me write this book?” the son imagined saying these words to his father.

What does it take to stop the generational curse? Can one go back in the generations and redeem the life that is left to live? 

After having his fill of minestrone, home-made beef jerky, and toasted pita bread coated with real butter,the middle-aged-man returned to the back room. It was there that he gave himself a peptalk.

“I am so tired. I need to sleep. I am exhausted. Depleted. I did not talk to my father about my current life. That is good. I did well. Everything is okay. It’s good. I didn’t have to defend myself. I didn’t have to compete with him. I allowed my Spirit to arise. We made peace. We allowed peace to arise from within ourselves.”
