The middle-aged-man texted his friend Tim

Disciple: “Available tomorrow?”

Timothy: for breakfast. I have to be at the dentist at 11am.

Disciple: sounds good. when and where?


The middle aged man laid his head back on his pillow. He held his cell phone close to his chest. He did not take off his clothes. He fell asleep right there. Do you see the middle-aged-man, dear reader? There he is. He is falling asleep. He is wearing the olive jacket.


Day 12
9:47PM

The son, who slept on a cushiony inflatable mattress woke at his father’s house later that evening. The television played loudly through the wall behind him. He sat, supported by two pillows in the half-lotus. His father’s dog, Spud, the little Chihuahua barked. The son was facing South, away from the sounds emanating from the living room. He read.

“To speak of wildness is to speak of wholeness. 
We humans come from that wholeness.” 
Zen Poet, Gary Snyder

The television stopped. The son could hear his father brushing his teeth. The son longed for the quiet of night. 

He searched youtube for a Bill Plotkin video. He found himself drawn to the North.

This is what Bill was saying on the youtubes:

North—healing. Building. Creating. Serving people and greater earth community. Eco-centric (nature centered). Member of the greater earth community. 

South---wild indegenous one. Loves emotions. Loves being human animal. Natural kinship with other species. Feels at home in other habitats. Instinctive. Sexual and erotic and loves being embodied.

East—innocent/sage. Equally innocent and wise. Pure clear sensing capacity. Experiences the big picture. Loves paradox. Appears as a sacred fool who doesn’t really care about the norms and rules of society. Trickster: the one who laughs at our extraordinary and weird human nature

West—muse beloved. Muse and inner beloved. Deeply imaginitive. Creative. Finds solutions and understandings that come from the depths. Loves the darkness. The unknown. Has a love affair with dreams, with unconscious, and undoing, and decaying of things. Death fully embraced. Most interested in the mysteries of the soul.

Plotking (Plot King? Hmmm…interesting…what is the plot?)… Plotkin says that we are born with a strength in only one of these areas but we must cultivate all of these facets of ourselves in order to be whole and healthy.

The middle-aged man recognized the reverence with which he had treated Timothy. Timothy had, at one time, been the embodiement of the Christ to a younger middle-aged-man. And through some course of miracles, the middle-aged-man was once again, able to recognize the Christ in his friend Tim. The younger man had taken such a resentment against fundamentalism that he’d been unable or unwilling to see Tim’s heart.

So, What’s it like to sit with that man, Timothy, and see his faults? Is that how we want to see this? Faults? Is there another way to ask that question? What’s it like to see Timothy as a man who is still maturing and to see places where Tim may be stuck in his progression? Maybe that’s not even the right way to say this. I’m hesitant to nail this down. What’s it like to love Timothy? I think I’m getting closer now. What’s it like to love that man? What’s it like to come back to him in love and acceptance? What’s it like to accept him as you accept yourself? What’s it like to see him in the context of spiral dynamics? What’s it like to feel him as an equal? What’s it like to know your way around your Self in his presence? Here’s one to try on for size…dare I say it?....What’s it like to have gone deeper than your mentor? Let’s try again. Let’s try it this way…What’s it like to know a bigger Christ than Tim knows? What’s it like to experience the Christ as Universal? What’s it like to have left the bible belt? Am I speaking foolishly now? Can I take these risks? Am I willing to risk trying out these statements? Can this be part of my process? What’s it like to love that man? I want to come back to that one.

Timothy still talks of the “saved” and the “lost”. “I asked him what those words meant to him, didn’t I?” the man questioned himself, although he already knew the answer.

Timothy had the check for the meal in his hand.

“I’ll get the tip,” said the younger man as he laid a twenty dollar bill down on the table.

“That’s not a tip,” said Timothy, batting the bill toward his old-disciple.

“Yes, it is,” said the younger man, “That’s my tip today. I’m sowing some seeds. I know you understand that.”

“Yes, I do,” said Timothy

The younger man noticed that, although his bank account was dwindling with no more money scheduled to come in, that he did not feel so scared as he laid the twenty down on the table. And yesterday, as he passed a ten dollar bill to a homeless-looking couple on the street corner, he felt no fear. He who gives up his life, gains it. He who gives up the money, gains his freedom from it.

“Okay,” said Tim. “I’ve got to get to this dentist appointment.” Several times during their conversation the silver-haired man had applied some anbesol to his gums.

“Yes,” said the middle-aged-man “let’s get you there.”

“Oh, wait!” said the younger man, “Could you please sign this for me?” He slid his new blue bible with the vine-textured cover over to Timothy.

Timothy took a few thoughtful moments to write in the front cover of the middle-aged-man’s new bible which had his name embossed in silver on the front.

“Okay,” said Timothy, “It was great to see you, my friend.

“Oh, no,” said the younger man, “I’m not leaving you yet. I want to get every moment I can with you. I’m so grateful that you took the time to sit down with me today.”

And so they waited in line. Timothy paid the bill.

As they turned toward the double doors, the younger man placed his arm over Timothy’s shoulder and spoke these words: “I’m here to blow you up Tim, because that’s what you’ve prayed for.” The two men laughed. These were words that were spoken with affection and humor. These two men had a history together. Timothy was with the young man when he was kicked out of his first church. Timothy and his family had held the young man as he as his family left the church of the bible belt to discover what was then a complete mystery, the Cosmic Christ. These two men were accepting the death and resurrection process. These two men were accepting the trembling that came with this process of inviting the Word into one’s very being. They were saying “yes” to life. They were saying yes to one another.

As they emerged from the Internation House of Pancakes into the morning light, they laughed and exchanged good-byes. They parted ways.

“Call me when it’s time to play!” The younger man was strumming his air guitar, walking backwards across the parking lot.

The man in the hooded olive jacket got in his little blue Toyota with the extremely cracked windshield. His direction seemed obvious. He was the hunter-gatherer  looking for the next berry. He was the following the herd. He turned his little car South-East down the Northwest Expessway. Then he turned South on Rockwell toward his old place of employment, where he hoped he would find his old friend and boss, Brian.

Inkling Design

The man is heading South on Rockwell. He is nostalgic. He knows these streets, or at least he once knew them. The people that he left behind here have been travelling these streets the whole time that he has been gone. He left this town like Moses left after he killed the Egyptian. Desert exile? Not exile. Refuge? Yes, refuge in the desert.  The man crossed through an intersection. What is the name of that street? It’s not wilshire, is it? Wilshire runs parallel to Rockwell, does it not. It’s the street that you enter the Bence’s neighborhood from. It’s the last main intersection that you cross through before you get to Brian’s shop when you’re coming from this way.

I’m awake. Sitting here in this room facing North. Trying to make sense. Is that what I’m trying to make sense? Maybe that’s a problem? I wanted to write “the” problem, but I felt that was a little too absolute. 

The man scrolled back down to the real time in which he belonged.


Day 13
December 31, 2019
11:37PM

The dying-author awoke just before the dawn of a new decade, a dawn that had already cracked on much of the world to his East--A midnight which had already come to many of his fellow human beings. The dying author acknowledged the new year in this way, crafting these sentences slowly as the minutes passed before him on the small clock in the lower right-hand- corner of his laptop. He celebrated the new year somberly, by himself, but not alone dear reader. He was mostly unafraid of what life was offering in this moment, and he felt grateful for it all.

His father’s house was quiet. The middle-aged man had napped for over three hours after another conversation with Kathy…

 The fireworks, those audible signs of the new year caused him to think of his children, and it seemed just right to send them a group text:

Father: “Happy New Year! Thinking of you as the dawn of a new decade arises upon us, my dear children! I heard the fireworks and thought, “I bet that’s what my kiddos are doing right now!” Peace to you and all our fellow human beings. Peace to this waking world and all its expressions of arising consciousness.”

The middle aged man admitted to himself his own loneliness. He had considered venturing out into the world earlier, but he had not. Instead, he asked Kathy about her synagogue. He had the conversation that his friend Jeremy advised him not to have just before he’d left the college-cow-town those five days ago.

 “So Kathy,” began the middle-aged-man, “you call your assembly place a synagogue? Is that correct? Am I getting that right?”

“Yes,” she said, “I call it a church sometimes so others can understand what I’m talking about.”

“Ahh, I see,” he responded, “And you find your peace there, participating in that form?”

“Yes, I do. Very much so. I have friends there. I enjoy the ceremonies. I feel supported there. I feel loved. I am cared for within those walls.”

“Oh, that sounds lovely, Kathy. I am so happy for you,” he encouraged her to continue.

“Yes, I love my synagogue and the people in it. We recognize the appropriate feasts as indicated in the old testaments. We do Davidic dancing…”

“Davidic dancing?” the man interrupted, “Do you mean that you dance with abandon?”

“Oh no, we don’t believe in that kind of thing, Davidic dancing is a structured group dance with very specific steps,” she explained, “It’s really beautiful. I’d have to show you a video.”

“Yes, I’d like to see that,” he said

She went on, “At our synagogue, we try to avoid the traditions of man by strictly observing the bible as it is written. Things go wrong when man comes in and tries to change things. When we stray from what’s written in the Word, that’s when things go wrong. We do our best to observe the Judaic traditions as they are set out in the Old Testament. The first Christians were Jewish people. If you just stick with the old testament, you can’t go wrong. You know, like Christmas, it’s a Pagan holiday, so we don’t celebrate that.”

“Do you ever find that you are inventing your own traditions despite your efforts to prevent them from coming in? I just ask because that was my experience as I did my best to dedicate myself to the holy scriptures,” he said.

“Oh yes, that’s always a danger. But we just stick to the Word. You can’t go wrong if you just stay with what’s written there.”

And so this interaction between the middle-aged-man and his father’s wife began. They found common ground in their recognition of the name that cannot be spoken, Y-W-H, the breath of God, the unspeakable name. They  also agreed that the annointed one was never called Jesus while he walked the earth, Yeshua being a closer representation of the name of the human-being that was called Messiah over two-thousand years ago. 

The middle-aged-man shared with her about the Universal Christ, the all inclusive One whose arms are wide open, the One who does not exclude anything or any one.

She responded, “I don’t understand this Universal Christ that you are talking about. That doesn’t make much sense to me.”

“That’s okay,” said the man, “I’m not trying to get you to believe anything. It’s okay with me that you don’t understand. My experience has lead me to see the Christ in everything and in every one. There is nowhere I can go to escape the all-encompassing embrace of Christ...”

The middle-aged-man dared to go on. He shared with her how he saw the Christ in his vipassana practice as he observed the breath, and how he saw the Christ in the Buddha. Then, he shared with her how he saw the Christ in his spirit animal, Snake, as he practiced therapeutic shamanism. He shared with her how he saw the Christ in the Hindu Gods and Goddesses.

She continued, “I just know that God said to have no idols before him. Moses went up on the mountain for forty days and the people formed a golden calf, because that’s what we do. We make false idols, and so that’s why I think it’s very important to follow the Word.”

“I think I understand what you are saying. There was a time in my twenties when I tried to live my life as I interpreted the scriptures literally,” He said.

“Well, what do you think of the bible? How do you see the scriptures? What do you believe?” she said.

The middle aged man paused. He began to explain. He stopped. He paused again, realizing how important this one was, for himself, that he be clear of any need to prove anything to Kathy. He was walking a fine line in this conversation. He tried to share only his experience, his strength, his hope. He did not want her to feel threatened or attacked.

“I can’t speak about what I believe, because belief never really helped me as much as I wanted it to,” he continued at last, “I can only speak about my experience. I once read the scriptures literally, and that lead to a type of madness, a death that came naturally as a result of that whole process.” 

The man backed up. He tried again. 

“Let me see if I can say this another way. Really, I just want to be held, Kathy. I never felt that I was held the way I needed to be. And my father was never held like he needed. And his father before him. And this is how I came into the church. I wanted to be held and I was told that I would be held by a literal interpretation of the bible. And I truly was held in that tradition, in that form, for a while. But, eventually, my literal understanding of the holy scriptures was not enough to hold me. When we sold all of our possessions, I gave myself to that form completely, but in the end, I didn’t feel held, so I felt that I had to move on. 
I moved on in my search. I moved on to find the next form, or practice, that would hold me. And this way of being fits me, maybe because I am one of those people that you might call an artist. It’s about the creative process for me. And that’s why it’s so important that I can see the Christ in the Buddha, or Snake, or the Hindu Gods and Goddesses. I never know where I’m going to see the Christ, and I can’t control where this provision of grace through the Cosmic Christ will come from. I know that these ideas may be disturbing to you, and I don’t mean to offend you at all, but I’m even going to take this a step further. You may need to cast me out, and if you do, I understand, but I’m going to speak some blasphemy right now, just to make a point. Here goes: Who is the enemy? Satan. What does Christ tell us to do for our enemies? Love them. So who do we love? We love Satan.”

The man stopped speaking. There was a long pause.

Finally Kathy speaks. “I just know that Jesus did a lot of casting out of demons. There is an enemy and I don’t think that we can love Satan. We have to fight Satan…”

“Okay,” said the man, “But what does the fight of a follower of Christ look like? Isn’t our only weapon love? How do we fight these evil principles and principalities? Do we literally put on God’s armour? And if so, what does that armour look like?”

“You can’t love Satan, that’s all I know.” She said.

“That very well may be so,” he said, “And fortunately I’m not trying to convince you of anything tonight. I’m just sharing my experience with you. I’m just using these words so that maybe we can both see something greater than ourselves. I’m being open and honest with you about my experience. I pray tonight, as I have been praying, that anything false in me will be burned up. Anything not genuine. Anything not authentic. I pray that these things will be burned up in me. And I thank you for your prayers of healing. I welcome them. I have suffered deeply, Kathy, and this is just who I find my Self to be today. I am aware of my need to be held by this all-inclusive, all-encompassing, Creative, Intelligent, and fully-Compassionate Universal Christ. Today I feel that I am in communion with this Christ. This is my experience. I hope that you can feel my respect for your experience today. I don’t need you to be anything different than what you are right now. I don’t need you to be anywhere different than where you are right now. Thank you for allowing me to stay in your home.

“I think I understand, Maybe we can just watch Edward Scissor-hands,” she suggested.

The man sat to watch…

Edward Scissor-hands. The young man who lied at the end of the cul-de-sac who was isolated from his community. The Avon lady came calling. She brought him home. The religious lady was afraid of him. He cut himself. He poked a hole in the water bed. He had trouble dressing and acting right. Everyone seemed curious at best--suspicious at worst.

But the middle-aged-man grew sleepy. He excused himself to his bedroom to nap, truly hoping that he would get to finish the movie another day. And this is the story of how the middle-aged-man woke up just twenty-three minutes before midnight and began writing as the decade passed before his eyes.

Day 14
3:12 AM

The writer gave his body a chance to sleep. He awoke from what he estimated to be a ten minute dream.

I am standing in a physical therapy gym. There is an older man in the parallel bars. He beckons me over. He is an older Asian man. He asks me a question. What is he saying? I am leaning in. I can’t understand him now, but I understood him when I was dreaming. He is telling me that he is unable or unwilling to do something that he said he would? Is that what it is? I stop him mid-sentence. I do not want to hear any more. I get the point. I understand. You have told me enough. I want you to stop talking. I am annoyed. No More explanation in necessary, old man. I don’t want to hear it any more. Why is your hand on my left thigh. I want you to remove your hand from my thigh. Then the question returns. The Question. There’s a deeper question. What is it? What are you asking me, old man? Something to do with physical therapy?  Why can’t I remember the question? I am listening.  Am i listening? I cannot hear the question now. I knew what it was then. I heard it. The man collapses. He goes to his knees outside the parallel bars. I look up at the crowd of my coworkers, there are many. Someone call 911! Everyones’ phones are out they are all calling at once. 911 will be flooded with calls. The cell phones make the faces of my coworkers glow. Everyone is dialing. “The patient is still breathing,” I announce. 

I awake in fear that I will have no insurance. I wake in fear of my rotting teeth. Tim’s toothache. I have no structure. Joseph’s words are in my ears, “how will you care for yourself?” Who will care for me? I am the overly-bearded man who shows up at Brian’s shop, my job is fading from me. What if I don’t return on the sixth for my Dr appointment. I have an appointment with Renita borders, my new therapist in a couple of days too. Do I go home now? Do I run home? Can I continue facing my father. My mother is calling, offering chowder and a “safer” place to be. My father’s house is a sad house where the televisions run constantly. I am very tired now. Depleted. Exhausted. My body is getting tired from never sleeping more than three hours at a time. I want to go home. Where is home? Father, what time is it? Mother invited me over for clam chowder tomorrow night. I want to see my mother before I go home. I am scared. I am lonely. I do not like the way the television sounds in this house. I do not like the isolation and sadness here. I am afraid that my father will be mad in the morning because I did not spend enough time with him yesterday. This is the home where people shut themselves in rooms and turn the television on all evening. This is the place they rarely leave. I want to go home. Where is home? Who will take care of me?

The writer sat on the light-tan leather couch in his father’s spare bedroom. He was facing North, toward the concentric circles at the head of the bed where he had just dreamed of the Asian man. The dying Asian man.? The Asian man who had a heart attack in the parallel bars. Was he from the East? The innocent sage? Of Bill Plotkin. The Trickster? The fool?

The writer scolled back to his writings from the day before. He wanted to tell the story of Brian and Nash and Inkling design.

The man sat on the light tan couch at 3:52AM. He faced North. He observed a breath. And another one. The Y-W-H. the observance of the I AM. He wondered if he was sinking too far into the soil, like he had done on Christmas day, one week ago. The one week anniversary of the Christmas day that the man was convinced of his death by the “lethal dose” of psilocybin mushrooms. 

The man had a story to tell. At least that’s what he thought he might be doing here. Or maybe he was just writing to save himself. Maybe he was writing so that he could be saved. That’s one question that he’d asked Tim yesterday.

“These words, ‘saved’ and ‘lost,’ what do they mean to you now after all these years?

The writer himself certainly felt saved these days. “Saved from what?” he mused of his own being. Was that honest? That last sentence. Was that an honest use of the word “muse.” It was moments like these when he remembered that writing had very little to do with writing and everything to do with that question that was famous within the realms of his own being, “How will I live with myself.”

The next question is sometimes this, “who is this self that you are trying to live with?”

Harbor Dave told the aspiring writer that he had written thousands of pages (or was it just a thousand that he’d estimated. I think it was just a thousand) that would never be published. The writer now realized that he had also written many pages that would never be published. 

“Do I have the courage to continue in this writing project even if it were to never be published?” he asked himself. Do I have the courage to call all these thought meaningless. Let’s try it for just a minute, just as ACIM has recommended on day 10. And day 11 which states, “My meaningless thoughts are showing me a meaningless world.” Ha! The greatest insult to a storyteller, right?

This made him think of his friend, Janice, back in Portales. The woman who he had once called muru (mutual guru). The woman who he’d cut out of his life after he read three-quarters of a book about codependencey. He stopped reading the book after he’d blocked his brother, his father, and three friends, including Janice, who he had been scheduled to meet with that morning.

He floundered now in this writing project, although he’d just completed writing a couple of significant chunks. His cell phone sounded a text. He leaned over to see who was contacting him at 4AM in the morning (that’s what his ex-wife used to say when she used the AM. He felt that it was redundant in nature to say AM and ‘in the morning.’ AM was sufficient. It bothered him that she did this over and over again. He felt that being an English teacher that she must be held accountable for these types of infractions). His cell phone signaled yet another text. That was three. This time, he really did reach over the couch to pick up the cell phone, which was plugged into the wall charging. There he goes. He’s about the reach.

It was his brother. The man was tired. His head felt foggy. He didn’t not know if he was ready to engage his brother right now. The man missed the showers at the adobe house on Avenue Q. The man wondered if he was sinking in to the soil. He wondered if he was too far gone to come back. “What is required of me to complete this process,” He wondered. “How far do I have (get) to go.” He wondered again. 

It was writing like this that connected him to something deeper than this writing project that he really did hope to get published. Oh, the impossibilty of that. Or rather, that felt like the impossible. How long had it been since the man had written of the miracles? The form was always passing through for the man. Transient form. Constantly changing. Ah, to be one of those people who finds a form and rests in it. This was not how the man experienced life. Even as he wrote, his form and voice were changing. They had to change in order for him to experience (there’s that word again) his own honesty. 

“Am I really ready to die this death?” he asked himself. (he wrote the phrase “he wondered to himself” but he sensed that he’d already used that phrase several times recently). Writing was a constant effort to not say the same thing, the same way, twice, especially in the same sentence or the same paragraph. And then, if he noticed that a word was used frequently (i.e. experience) then it must somehow be acknowledged. He wondered what he would find if he looked up the word “experience” in a thesaurus. 

What about Stan and David in Cloudcroft? Was that a place he needed to be? Was there something there for him? Was there some “experience” that he needed in Cloudcroft? Why was that word experience so important. “Experience is important because it is through experience that we experience the change that we so deeply need,” first person writer answered third-person writer. “You just used the word “experience” twice in that one first-person sentence…no…actually three times.” The writer’s head was spinning. He opened google to find a thesaurus.

The writer needed to feel the warmth of a shower. He wanted to feel the warmth of the shower. He did, as the writer’s father slept just a few feet away, he showered. It was a mainly uneventful shower, mostly just the cleaning of his body. He did, however, sit down for a bit and feel the warm water as he allowed himself to come into contact with this breath and his body (btw, “contact” was one of the synonyms for “experience” in the thesaurus). As he dried off he remembered one of the teachings of his Pema, which was “Go to the places that scare you.” Was that not the teaching of the Christ when he asked the rich young man to sell all of his possessions and give to the poor? Was the Christ not asking the rich young man to “go to the places that scared” him? 

“Oh,” thought the writer, “a shower in close proximity to my father. I think that qualifies as one of those places. Could it be possible after all these years that when you are around your father you are still a scared child?”

The writer suddenly wanted to run back to the comfort of the medicine. “The comfort of the medicine!?” he thought loudly. “Are you serious? The medicine that made you feel like you were going to die?” Now the writer was answering himself in second person. They say that’s a sign of true insanity. “Who is ‘they’?” the writer questioned.

“Pema?” the writer asked….

Let’s try that again…

“Pema?” the son asked

“Yes, son?” said the Pema

“How long do I stay in these places that scare me?”

“Pema?”

“Yes, Son?”

“What time is it?”

“Do you remember when you went to the mountains?” asked his Pema.

“Which time?” the son asked (knowing exactly which time she was referring to).

“That time that you went all by yourself, son. That time that you drove all the way up to the Santa Fe National forest by yourself. Your son wasn’t with you and that was a pretty big deal, and you knew the deal would get even bigger in your heart once you got there. And do you remember how, on the trail register, someone had written, ‘watch for cougars! Stay with your party!’. And you were all alone, weren’t you, son?”

“Yes, Pema, I was,” Said the son.

“And how did that make you feel,” asked his Pema.

“I felt scared, my Pema,” confessed the son.

“And what did you do?” she asked.

“I signed the trail register and I started my backpack adventure.”

“Yes, that is how I remember it too,” she said.

“And what happened next, son,” Pema asked.

The son responded, “I hiked and hiked all day long. I was all by myself and the sun was shining down on me in the forest as I hiked through the changing landscape. I hiked through tall grasses. I hiked past all different kinds of wild flowers and dandelions. I hiked through open flat fields, and rocky ascents. I hiked past large rocks. I hiked past uprooted trees. I hiked past tall, sticky pines. I saw a horny toad. I rested, eating packets of tuna. I hiked through green ferns and aspens. And the whole time I wondered about feared I was being hunted by a cat. Well, not the whole time, I finally got too tired to care much about the cougar that might be stocking me. But, she was there with me the whole time. And I got so tired. I got tired and a lot of emotions came up and I started crying and hiking at the same time. Then my tears turned into prayers. And I came to that place where I didn’t want to go any further, and I wondered why in the heck I’d come in the first place, but by then I had gone too far to turn back…”

“Right there,” said Pema, “That’s exactly how far you have to go. You get to go so far out that you can’t come back. That’s what they call ‘past the point of no return.’ That’s the place that scares you. And that’s the only place where you can make friends with your fear. It’s right there, you’re all alone with it, so you have no choice but to work with yourself. That’s the place where your practice is born. So, are you there yet?”

“I don’t know, Pema. Am I there yet?” the son felt like he was in the back seat of Pema’s car crying, “Are we there yet, Pema?”

“Just keep talking about what happened next, son.”

“Thank you,” said the son.

“What are you thanking me for?” said his Pema.

“For calling me son?” said the son.

“You are very welcome. That’s what you are, isn’t it? A son?”

“Yeah, I know,” said the son, “but I know that you are a non-theist and the ‘son’ thing, well that’s kind of my thing and not yours.”

“I am the Universal Pema,” she said, straightening her yellow Pema robes, “I AM all forms, and I am no forms. Now get on with your story, because you’ve got a point to make here.”

“Yes, Pema, I will.” Said the Universal Son. “I had ascended past the point of no return, up past ten-thousand feet now. I was all by myself. I hadn’t seen another hiker all day. I cried as I walked with the pack on my back. I cried for my own loneliness. I missed my son. Hre was my trail partner. We’d been together on every trip to the mountains. We helped each other get our packs on and off and talked to one another and watched out for one another, and he wasn’t there. And that loneliness pressed in on me.

And all day I had wondered about the large cat that might be sneaking up behind me. And sometimes I stopped and I listened very carefully, and I turned around to look behind me, scanning the trees and underbrush for any movement or color difference. And the more tired I got, the more intense the sense of loneliness and loss became. It was grief, that’s what I was feeling so intensely. Grief: when your happiest memories become your most painful ones; that’s how I had come to define it for myself, and miss Du’bois, at work, when I explained it to her that way, she told me that I should write a book. So there I was, dealing with my loss. Iwas past the point of no return and the grief was mounting up. And I kept turning around to see if there was a big cat behind me, knowing that any big cat worth their salt would never give away its position to a lumbering human with a forty pound pack on his back. I knew I would never see her coming. And I didn’t have a knife, but I did have my dragon-tooth necklace, and when that moment came I planned to gouge her in the eyes with my dragon tooth necklace. I don’t know what that was about because I did have a knife in my pack, yet still I planned to gouge the attacking cougar with my dragon-tooth necklace that the mountain climber from the Life Healing Center had made just for me. 

My tears, at some point turned into prayers. I prayed for my ex-wife and I prayed for the woman from Bermuda who I had put on a plane—the woman who I had left my ex-wife for. That thing I had done that I was still coming to terms with. This was before I knew that Sapphos had returned to Lesbos island. Anyway, I prayed for these women long and hard. I asked God that these women would be given the strength to recover from the harm that I had done to them. I especially remember praying for the woman from Bermuda who I wanted so deeply, the woman that I tried so hard to give myself away to, but something inside of me would just not allow it, no matter how hard I tried. It was for her that I remember praying for the most fervently. Up and up I went that day.

I came with the intent that night of meeting Kali Ma on the mountain. She is a Hindu Goddes with blue skin and at least four arms who cuts mens head off with a scythe. I was praying for the death of my false self. The head represented the ego to me. And it was with the specific intention of making love to Kali Ma in this way that I had come to the mountain.

After nine hours of physical and emotional exertion, I arrived at lake Johnson at over eleven-thousand feet elevation. I made my camp. The making of that camp was one of the loneliest and saddest things I had ever done. There were no other campers up there. My son was not with me. I was well past the point of no return. I was in up to my neck, the neck that I prayed Kali Ma would sever that night. I pitched the tent. I prepared wood for the fire. I cooked some shrimp flavored Ramen. I rolled out my sleeping bag. I got everything organized for the night. Having completed all the camp chores, I was ready to go meet her.

 
I knew where I was going. My son and I had been to this very lake just two weeks before. I followed the stream up into the forest, past where anyone might find me if something were to go wrong. There was a lush, green, ground covering up there. There were these giganitic boulders too, with mysterious bodies of water around them. A couple of these boulders were gigantic, fifteen or twenty feet high. 

These boulders had spirits in them. I could feel that. And the green ground covering too. And the stream. It was all alive. It was all breathing. And there was the spirit of the cougar there with me the whole time, the one who stalks me. She was there. She was always there. There was one place where when I stepped, the ground sounded hollow. I had on my black combat boots, the same pair that I would leave behind at the ENMU brick wall when I took my hitchike months later. Anyway, these black combat boots, when I stomped the ground made this incredibly strange thunking sound in the earth. I began stomping and dancing. I began calling on the spirit of Kali Ma. “Kali Ma! Kali Ma! Kali Ma! I yelled out time and time again. Her name rang through the valley. And when I stopped calling her name the quiet was just as loud. And I thought that she would surely come in the form of a the mother cat. 

And it was in this way that I summoned the spirit of Kali Ma, saying yes to the death that I knew I must die. And it was there also that I cried out the name of the others that had helped me. I called on the name of Yeshua. I called on the Buddha. I called on Shiva and Shakti, the male and the female God and Goddess. It was in this way that I made myself vulnerable that night on the mountain. It was in this way that I made myself vulnerable in both spirit and in my physical being. 

I found my way back to the quiet camp as the sun was setting. It was dusk and getting duskier. I lit the great stack of firewood that I had made from large branches and the trunk of a tree. The fire was nestled where two large rocks met. It was sheltered from the wind. And I saw that there was a curious animal hole right there by my camp. It was a tunnel that went into the earth. Yes. I was very interested in that hole. 

I drank water. I ate more food. The sky became blue above the trees in the valley. The fire roared. I fell sound asleep. When I woke up the fire was out. That’s when I heard the mysterious clicking sound that I spoke of earlier—the strange clicking from which would eventually arise the Exquisite Stillness.

“Pema?” said the son, “Are you there?”

“Ask him to take you to see the cats,” said Pema.

“The cats?” said the son (knowing exactly the cats that she was talking about)

The writer recalled how he’d realized that he was a Hindu, and how he had eventually come to call upon Kali Ma that day on the mountain after he followed the small stream up to the large boulders which rested among the thick and sprouting green beings of the soil:

As the man continued his awakening process over the years he gained many tools. He read many books and integrated the life experience of the “other” into his own practice. After Penny had shared with him that he was “one of the top five self-bludgeoners” that she’d ever met, the man tried to discover for himself the love that he so desperately needed. Penny gave him a book: The Pocket Pema Chodron. He read a book by Thich Nhat Hanh called The Art of Power.  From these two great teachers he came to experience the power of silence. He began to observe on a deeper level what was actually going on in his mind and in his body. He also rediscovered a medicine in his beloved marijuana plant.

The writer found this song upon his lips:

“There’s a beautiful green leaf that grows upon the earth. 
It helps us to sing and encourages mirth
But the man says no and there’s a reason why
He doesn’t want us to see what we can see when we are high”

The herb connected him with himself. He was able to experience joy, once again, in a world that seemed to be crushing him. In a marriage where he often times felt guilty for being himself, the marijuana came to him and offered him peace and love and joy within himself. It also gave him ample opportunity to face his fears. The chronology of all of this was impossible for the writer to untangle as he sat at his mother’s home writing on the Thursday morning, the second day of the year two-thousand-and-twenty. But he saw the healing process that had happened in his heart.

There was Thich Nhat Hanh first. The vietnamese monk who had watched so many of his friends and family die in the war that ravaged his country. The man who had been exiled from his country. The man who taught his readers to breathe. The man who described the beauty and the simplicity and the freedom that may be experienced in the breathing-in and the breathing-out. It was in this book that was given to his wife by a great icelandic princess that the man discovered that he was breathing and that it mattered. The awakening that happened in the heart of the buddha showed the way to the breath and the universal consciousness that we may all experience as we turn our attention toward that simplest of all human experience which we all have in common. The breath, that form which none of us will abandon until our body finally stops breathing. 

It was Thich Nhat Hanh who first helped the man to see this. And then there was Pema in this little book that Penny gave him. Pema was simple and digestable. She kept bringing him back to the here and the now. She kept urging the awakening man to go to the places that scared him. She invite him to let things be when he thought they needed to change to his liking. She invited him to accept his birthright, the gift of this moment, that which is beyond the resolution that we all think will finally satisfy us. She invited him to sit with his loneliness. She dared him to discover himself and to accept himself and his attitudes and his thoughts for exactly what they were. She gave him permission to just allow himself to be. She gave him permission to stop trying to fix himself. She asked him to speak to his fear with respect. She wondered what would happen if he just stuck his head right in that demons mouth instead of trying to cast him out. She invited him to go deeper when the world was telling him to go higher. She asked him to sit as fear was screaming in his face, trying to tell him what he had to do. She told a simple story of the Tigers and the Strawberries which went something like this:

A young woman who is walking through the forest when discovers that she is being pursued by tigers. She runs as fast as she can to escape the terrifying creatures who chase her. As she runs she comes to a cliff. She climbs down a vine which is growing down the cliff-face. As she descends the vine she looks down. Below her she sees yet another group of growling tigers. “Oh my,” says the young woman to herself, “there are tigers above me and below me. What will I do now?” It is in this moment that she notices a little family of mice above her. The mice are gnawing through the vine which holds her there suspended above and below the hungry tigers. In this very moment she sees something else. It is a little strawberry plant. A single berry grows from the plant’s green body. The woman reaches out. She picks the berry. She smells the fruit. She eats that strawberry.

Yes. That is the end of that story. The story with the non-ending. The story without resolution. The story with a happy ending. The story with a tragic ending. The story of the woman who reaches out and plucks the strawberry from the vine. Praise be to the tigers and the strawberries and the mice who gnaw on the vine. Praise be to the one who eats the strawberry.

It was in this way that the man began his meditative practice. It was in this way that the man found himself eating every strawberry that he found as he dangled above and below the tigers that chased him through life. It was in this way that the man found a new teacher and he dedicated himself to her teachings. He carried his Pema around with him in the right side-pocket of his cargo pants. It was in this way that the man continued to make a deeper peace with himself and the world in which he lived. Praise be to the Pema who asks for no such praise, the one who did not hold herself accountable to any deity.

And so it was inscribed on the inside cover of the pocket Pema:

To Travis,
Be the one you dream you can be.
Penny

And it was in this way that Pema and Penny became the same being to him. It was in this way that the man also gave himself to the teachings of Penny who always gave him unconditional positive regard (Praise be to the one who was called Carl Rogers!).

The doorbell rang at his mother’s house. The man wondered if it was his father who had finally come to put that bullet in his head. He heard no violence as his mother’s husband answered the door. He heard no gunshots. He chose, once again, the strawberry that was before him.

The man was never fully faithful to the Vipassana practice given to him. He phased in and out. This man was never faithful at anything, even the things that he claimed to love the most, like his music or his family. But, the man did continue.

It was in this way that the man found himself on occasion sharing this joy with the world. He and two friends started a meditation group. They read together. They sat together. They talked together. They read again.They sat again. They talked again. And that was their form. That was the form that they held together. Together, they gently practiced and learned from one another. They learned to listen. They learned to give space for what was said instead of trying to fix things or give advice. They gave each other the gift on one another’s presence. It was in this way that they practiced on-again and off-again for four years, sometimes drinking tea, sometimes just meeting for breakfast, sometimes reading TNH, sometimes reading Joseph Campbell.

Please allow these words to hasve their effect. The mountain is high. The valley is low. A heart that is turned inward toward you.

And this is how the son continued his practice on-again and off-again not understanding why the man’s father would ever think he was a Hindu, and not at all thinking he was a Hindu and not even really very aware at all of the historical connection between Buddhism and Hinduism. Pema certainly never mentioned any Hindu Gods or Godesses, she herself describing her worldview as non-theist (not anti-theist because there was no position against anything that would bring true peace into being).

And then it came to pass that months later the man found himself talking to an old friend in front of the Wal-Mart with whom he was sharing his most recent spiritual awakening (for there had certainly been many in his precious and sacred lifetime). The old friend was very glad that the man was doing well and she shared with him how she had been concerned about his well being for quite some time.
“I heard that you had become a Hindu,” she whispered to the man as they stood there beside the sliding doors in front of the Wal-Mart.

“That’s strange,” thought the man, who honestly recoiled at the idea himself. “I do not understand why people think that I am a Hindu.”

And so it came to pass that the younger man went home where he brought his groceries in from the little blue Toyota, unpacked them, and made himself a nice lunch of lentil soup and strawberry shortcake with coconut milk for lunch. A meal that fit his current vegan diet (a diet that he made no vows to continue forever, but one which he currently embraced with a joyful and appropriate zeal).

It was in this way that he found himself doing a google search for “Hinduism,” after which he found himself reading Wikipedia which helped him to understand this: The name Hindu was not a name that was adopted by any one people or religious group. It was actually a description that foreigners assigned to the people who generally lived by the Indus River, “Indus” meaning a great body of water, in this case a great flowing body of water. 

It was in this simple way that the man munching on lentil stew, at that moment, as he sat in front of the computer in the great adobe house, came to understand that he how he truly was a Hindu. He was one of a Universal people who draw their Life from the Great River. 

And it was in this way that the man became curious about Hinduism (however broad that spectrum may be). It was in this way that he came to ask Shiva, the divine feminine to arise from within him, to uncoil the great serpent that he had felt quivering at the root of his being. It was in this way that he called for a marriage of the divine masculine, who was Shiva, to marry the divine feminine, Shakti in his being. It was in this way that he continued to see the diving in all that is. It is in this way that he came to see the Christ in the Hindu gods and goddesses. It is in this way that he met Kali Ma and asked her to come and couple with him. It is in this way that he was aroused by the possibility of backpacking by himself up to a mountain lake which was fed by a stream which he would follow as far as he could, at which point he would stomp the ground and summon the great Kali Ma to come and destroy him. It was in this way that he would eventually (ironically?) rediscover this prayer.

11:15AM

The writer checked his emotions, then he checked his motivations, meaning that he allowed Presence to greet them both. He allowed his innate presence, that essence of his being to welcome all those parts of his being. 

He faced South into beveled edge of the mirror that showed a repeating image of the lover par of his face, that wild and bearded aspect of his being. His brother, in a recent text exchange between the two had expressed how much he wished he could grow a beard like his older brother. And there certainly were interactions between his beard and the world which encouraged him to allow its continued growth. But it was the man’s relationship with his own beard that was the most important to him.

The beard had been given permission to expand into its current state of large-ness over a year ago as the man left the psychiatric unit in Roswell and enrolled himself in the treatment center in Rio, Racho. The middle-aged-man called it his homeless-beard, and he freely spoke of its significance whenever the chance presented itself.

“This is my homeless-beard,” the man found himself professing, “I am considering buying a one-way-ticket to the big island of Hawaii. If I’m going to be homeless I don’t want to be cold.” These words drew a mixed response of fear, confusion, and smiles from his listeners as he spoke them in varying degrees of detail and length.

After his recent hitchhike to Amarillo, the man did not think that he was ready to become willingly homeless just yet. If that was the path, the Spirit would need to do some more work in him. He did not enjoy the experience of not knowing where he would sleep safely that night---the first time he’d ever known such a thing. He let the door close quickly once as he investigated an abandoned office complex where it appeared someone had already taken up residence. It was not in his plans to go back there.

Hitchiking that day, sitting on the side of the road, waiting for a ride that would never be offered, his hands getting colder and colder gave him just enough understanding of whatever that was to know that he was not ready to go there willingly. Yet today, he was willing to stay at his mother’s house, passing a waypoint on the non-path to the -point-of-no-return, and what that point was he was not quite clear on. It was in this way that he found himself facing South, his face split in the beveled edge of a large mirror (though not near as large as the one in the kitchen of the adobe house).

In his book Wild Mind, Bill Plotkin identified the South as the Wild Indigenous One. “Is this the beard of the Wild Indigenous One that I am seeing in the mirror this morning?” wondered the dying author. He had read a book once by Robert Bly, called Iron John. He remembered the hairy wild man as being a main impetus of the message in that book. The writer was coming to accept that this South-facing position was no coincidence, but rather a deeper wisdom that had brought him into this relationship with the wildness of his beard. He pulled up the shades on the West facing window, acknowledging his Beloved Muse (Plotkin). 

His beloved muse, that one who dives into the mystery of the she-serpent within, who is curious about the upside-down cross, The one who wants to document the process of moving past the point-of-no-return. The one who calls upon Kali Ma. The one who revels in the experience and the danger and the fear of walking away from the God-of-culture, attempting to integrate the most ancient of non-religions, shamanism. 

The man was now beginning to acknowledge all four of the cardinal directions. In the mirror he could see the North wall of the bedroom in which he stood. The North, where the generous and caring mature adult watched over, inquiring about the man’s personal well being, while also concerned for the well-being of his family, the world, and the health of the planet.

The East, where his innocent sage lived and breathed deeply. This is the direction from which his teacher, Thich Nhat Hanh had arrived in him. The innocent/sage. The one who is child-like sometimes in naivete, yet also carries the wisdom of the ages in the manifestation of this innocence. Also, the East is where the trickster comes from, this one whom he saw so strongly in his daughter, this one who is ready to let go of the serious nature of life and become playful in whatever format is available. The trickster who could easily find herself transmuted into the sacred fool, that one who intentionally steps into trouble in order to stir the metaphorical pot, allowing the dross to rise to the top and the Truth to condense.

According to our friend, Bill, we each have one Cardinal direction that is innately our strong-suit. And it is the direction directly behind us as we embody our great strength that is the weakest. As the writer took his lesson from the wisdom-voice which seemed to be writing this book, it appeared that his primary direction was the North. It appeared that it was this nurturing adult who had brought him this far in his journey. It was his nurturing adult who was introducing him to these other aspects of his being. It was the generative nurturing adult who was bringing all of them together and caring for these fragmented aspects of the man’s being. It was the nurturing adult who had showed up yesterday at his father’s house as he raged against his son, trying to bring him into submission with insults. “Yes,” thought the man, “Praise be to the nurturing adult that lives in me, this one who cares for me, this one who cares for the world.”
However, today the writer faced South into the mirror with the beveled edge that split the bottom half of his face in two. He faced his wild-man beard, the beard that even his wild-man brother was jealous of. The writer sensed an opportunity to nurture this wild-man of the South. He would rely on the North to help him to develop his South, for it was from the North that his natural strength flowed. 

Was it the Wild-Inidigeous one who searched for shelter in Amarillo? Was it the Wid-Indigenous one who perceived the life process as a hunter-gatherer? Was it his southern nature that found the nuts and berries and followed the herd. Was it his wild indigenous nature who was the fisher-of-men and fish alike? Was it this nature that desperately needed to find some expression in order for the man to live in a healthy way? Was it for the reviving of this nature that the man had come to this place in his life? Was it that southern nature that the man had gone to the forest to recover, or possibly discover? Was it this wild nature that brought him to his father’s house, to face the wild-man of his youth himself? Was it his wild nature that caused him to move toward what scared him?

The writer remembered his practice at the adobe house in the college-cow-town, when he stood at the counter facing North, with the pillows behind him at the sink, in dialogue with this being. He wondered if he could do the same here, in his mother’s house facing South. He got the pillows off the bed and placed them on the floor behind him. He sat. He brought his hands together and placed his thumbs on his forehead. He bowed toward his heart. In silence. In prayer.

He bowed. He prayed. He acknowledged the South-One. As he moved into a pose that he thought felt wild and indegenous, knees bent, feet forward, forearms resting on his knees, spine straight and ready—a position of restful readiness, he became aware. He became aware of the bedroom furniture. He became aware that his books and his laptop were resting on the same bedroom furniture that had inhabited his parent’s room when he was a child.

The tall dresser in the East wall was where his father kept a growing stack of playboy magazines. The magazines that the middle-aged-man, when he was teen-ager, used to masturbate and presumably the same magazines that his father also used to masturbate, a strange bond that the writer had not, until now, been aware of. He had never imagined that his father used the same magazines to pull his own off.

The writers’ step-father (by which name he had never referred to him before) poked his head in and gave him a key to the house. He was running out to do some errands. “Make sure you don’t run off with that key, now,” He said. The step-son did his best to not read anything in to that imperative. 

The writer recalled how he used to have his favorite magazines, and the joy that he felt when a new magazine magically appeared in his father’s dresser. The writer walked over to the cabinet, opening the familiar door. There were three shelves inside. It was empty now. The writer checked several of the other drawers in the wooden cabinets. the drawers in the bedroom set were mostly empty.

It was on the top shelf where the magazines were kept. When the writer, in his young adult life, had become a born again Christian he questioned the presence of those magazines of his youth on a moral level. He understood the magazines to be wrong. He understood them to be of a sinful nature. He did not question how he had come to this conclusion. It just seemed to fit. And so the young Christian vowed to never touch the dirty magazines again that had lurked so darkly on the upper shelf of his father’s dresser. 

The writer stood waiting, facing South as he waited for the punch-line to all of this. This was a story that he’d told before. Did his southern nature have some more to add to this story? “What does the Wild-Indigenous one have to say?” the writer spoke out loud. He waited. He took a seat on the pillows behind him and he waited.

“What was sex like,” wondered the writer, “before a simile of sex could be experienced by looking at an image of a perfect specimen in the pages of a magazine? What was sex like before the act of intercourse could be viewed as quickly as it took to say, “hey google? Maybe this is about more than sex. What was life like before there were wooden drawers in which to store playboy magazines? What is life lik for the wild-indegenous one? What is life like for this being who exists in here who does not quite understand all of the progress that has been made and how ‘good’ it is for us all? What is life like for this being who has been forced to wake by the clock? What is life like for this being who is now required to be at the same location for forty hours a week and fifty-two days a year in order to survive? What does these kind of restrictions do to this being? What is it like for a being to wake up to his own sexuality with a picture of a woman in contrast to an actual woman? What is it like for this being who evolved to hunt and to gather to be confined in these ego-structures and finding his sex in magazines and on-line? 

Dear reader, I do not intend to romantacize hunter-gatherer culture. I know that we are not going back. I am just asking these questions in an effort to reach out to this wild-indigenous one as a generative-nurturing adult. The southern nature of my being is the one at my back, the weakest of my being. So, how does this work? Where do I find myself today?

He had been writing since early that morning with only a short break to shower and eat.
But, he thought that he’d give something on more try.

When the writer was a young boy, his parents brought him to church. They all attended church for a short while. The young boy attended a class in which they taught him why he was to be baptized. All the boy could remember of the class later was the story of how melted gold from the golden calf was poured down the throats of the disobedient Israelites who had formed an idol while moses was away getting the ten commandments. The writer did not even know now how accurate that story was.

The young man sat with his family one Sunday morning at the church service. The wooden pews were cushioned by a plush velvet seat. Light streamed through the stained glass windows of the First Christian Church. The boy sat next to his father, listening to a sermon. Kyle Maxwell, the pastor/preacher told a story that the man still remembers.

The story is of a man whose house has been drowned in a flood. The man is sitting on his roof, waiting for God to rescue him. A large log floats by. “I don’t need that log,” says the man, “I’m waiting for the Lord to rescue me.” Then, a man in a row boat comes by. “Can I help you?” says the man in the row boat. “No, thank you,” says the man, “I’m waiting for the Lord to rescue me.” Finally a rescue helipcopter spots the man sitting on the roof. The pilot shines a bright light on the man and communicates with the man through a loudspeaker. “YOU. DOWN THERE!” says the helicopter pilot. “WE WILL LOWER DOWN A BASKET AND HAUL YOU UP!” “NO THANK YOU,” yells the man politely, “I’M WAITING ON THE LORD TO RESCUE ME.” The helicopter lowers the basket. The man refuses to get in. The helicopter raises the basket and flies away in order to rescue others.
The flood waters rise around the man. He is washed away and drowns.

When the man gets to heaven the man asks the Lord. Why didn’t you save me God? I was putting my trust in you. “Well son,” says the Lord, “I sent a log. I sent a row boat. And I sent a helicopter, and you didn’t accept my help in any of these things.”

I honestly, now do not know why I am telling this story. I’ve been writing for a long time and when I started writing, this story was in my mind. I thought that I would somehow come full circle. I hoped that somehow God would do a miracle here. Maybe I’m missing the log. 

I suppose that this is a story about how…I’m tired…I need to rest

Maybe this is not where the herd is headed. Maybe there are just no nuts or berries to find on this path. Maybe sometimes we have to walk for a ways down a path in order to finally turn back around. But here I am, participating in this form. Here I am. Praise to the directionless story. Praise to the fool who has no idea where he is going. Praisse to the wild-indigenous one who goes out searching. Praise to the Scout who looks for a new land. Praise be to the all. Praise be to the non-path.

Long pause…5 minutes or longer…

Dear reader, minutes have passed since I last wrote, thank you for bearing with me. Thank you for coming with me on this journey. As I was unable to see any connection in the thoughts I have shared wayou over the last several pages, I must admit that I began to despair. It is incredible how quickly a heart can fall into despair. It is incredible how quickly I, in particular, can fall into despair. 
whates
I will attempt to recount what happened here. I closed my laptop, as I said, despairing in the meaninglessness of what I had written. I went as far as wondering if I had chosen incorrecty in my choice to stay here in Oklahoma City this morning. And in the recounting of the story of the man and the flood, I felt very nearly crushed by this practical voice of wisdom which might come from someone more spiritually minded, but less abusive than my own father. I felt sick in my spirit. I began backtracking, just barely falling short of questioning God as to where I went wrong. I returned a text to my friend, kalei who was inviting me for steaks tomorrow night at his house, thinking, “Oh yeah, I definitely missed it! Look there’s the log floating by my submerged home. There’s the Lord trying to save me, trying to get me back home so that I can salvage whatever little bit may be left of my life. And it is possible that all of this is true! I can’t really say for sure. But here is what happened next.

I opened ACIM to lesson 13, which reads as follows: 

“A meaningless world engenders fear.” 

ACIM goes on to say, “Recognition of meaninglessness arouses intense anxiety in all the separated ones. It represents a situation in which God and the ego “challenge” each other as to whose meaning is to be written in the empty space that meaninglessness provides. The ego rushes in frantically to establish its own ideas there, fearful that the void may otherwise be used to demonstrate its own impotence and unreality. And on this alone it is correct.

ACIM continues, “It is essential, therfor, that you learn to recognize the meaningless, and accept it without fear. If you are fearful, it is certain that you will endow the world with attributes that it does not possess, and crowd it with images that do not exist. To the ego, illusions are safety devices, as they must also be to you who equate yourself with the ego.

The writer wondered, “Does the Wild-Indigenous one experience meaningless? What is meaningless to the Wild-Inidgenous one? Does the indigenous one pass up the help of a floating log because it has too little meaning?” 

“Who is this wild one? Who is this indigenous one?” the writer continued in his musings. “What does ‘meaning’ mean to this indigenous one? Is it the Wild-One that can carry us through when meaning ceases to be apparent? Is there one within us who is not demanding for anything to be meaningful? Is there One who just simply is? Is there One within us who does not interpret the lack of nuts or berries or the lack of game as the lack of meaning? Is there one who senses the emptiness (the void) and simply walks on, eyes and ears alert, accepting whatever may seem to be currently unavailable?”

The writer wondered about this woman who wrote ACIM. He thought about the rising of the feminine in this world. He thought about how the masculine nature served humanity in its early stages, but now it appears that the feminine nature is rising from within to wake us up. The she-snake that is unwinding within us, working its way up the crown chakra of humanity.

The writer wondered how the perfect images of naked female beings in the magazines had affected him. He recalled the phrase “objectfication of the female body,” that he’d come to understand as he came into some understanding, years ago, into what it meant to be a feminist. 

He cracked the window a bit to let some fresh air in. He thought he might have heard the voice of his new friend Sebastian, one of the two boys who had worked a miracle for him yesterday as he was struggling to recover from the interaction with his father. His mother said that everyone called him “Sebby.” The middle-aged-man like that name, Sebby. Praise be to the boys on power-scooters who drive up and down the street, bringing salvation to the world!

Dear Reader, It is I, the one who narrates. I AM. The One who tells this story. The one who sees the Universal in our man here. It can be a challenge for everyone when I show up. It can be a challenge for others when the Universal appears in a being. It was a challeng for those in the Near East when it happened over there all those years ago. Maybe we can make some peace with that together. 

That which is written can never be complete. That which is spoken can never be removed from its speaker, and this is why the Annointed one didn’t write his own book. And why, you may ask were the gospels written so many years after the one they called Messiah walked the earth? The answer is in these words somewhere if you will search your own heart carefully. Are you ready? Here they are:

Some time needed to elapse between the happening and the telling. In human terms we are talking about anywhere from thirty-five to eighty years. That’s quite a bit of time between the happening and the telling, plenty of time for a lot of things to happen to those memories. Plenty of time the heart to get a hold of what had happened to do with it exactly what a human heart does.

Having been moved by these words, the writer opened to the book of Luke. He read of Zecaraiah and Elizabeth, and Mary and how the ange Gabriel announced the coming of John and the annointed one. The writer, having become sleepy laid down for a nap.
